# ->THE TRIVULZIO CODEX<-
<note>original: "Il Codice Trivulziano". This manuscript, dated primarily to 1487–1490, is celebrated for its extensive lists of vocabulary intended to improve Leonardo's literary expression, as well as his architectural and military drawings.</note>

### ->BY<-

# ->LEONARDO DA VINCI<-

<term>The Trivulzio Codex</term>, Leonardo da Vinci
<note>This specific notebook was acquired by the aristocratic Trivulzio family in the 18th century and is currently preserved at the Biblioteca Trivulziana in Milan’s Sforza Castle.</note>

<summary>This is the formal title page of the manuscript, identifying the work as the Trivulzio Codex and attributing it to Leonardo da Vinci, accompanied by historical archival shelfmarks.</summary>
<keywords>title page, Leonardo da Vinci, Trivulzio Codex, archival marks, Milan, Renaissance manuscript, Biblioteca Trivulziana</keywords>

### LUCA BELTRAMI
<note>Luca Beltrami was a renowned Italian architect and historian. He is famous for restoring the Sforza Castle in Milan, where this manuscript is now housed.</note>

<note>A red-ink reproduction of a profile study by Leonardo da Vinci, depicting an elderly man with a prominent nose, sunken mouth, and a soft cap. Leonardo frequently drew these "character studies"—sometimes called grotesques—to investigate the diverse forms of human anatomy and the effects of aging on the face.</note>

->REPRODUCED IN 94 HELIOGRAPHIC PLATES<-
<note>Heliography was a 19th-century photographic process used to create high-quality, permanent prints of artworks. This allowed scholars to study the manuscript without handling the fragile original.</note>

->BY<-

### LUCA BELTRAMI
<note>Luca Beltrami (1854–1933) was a distinguished Italian architect and historian. He is best known for his meticulous restoration of Milan’s Sforza Castle (Castello Sforzesco), where the original manuscript of the Trivulzio Codex is preserved today.</note>

<note>The original page features a red-ink reproduction of a profile study by Leonardo da Vinci. It depicts an elderly man with characteristic "grotesque" features—a prominent nose and sunken mouth. Leonardo used these studies to explore the extremes of human facial anatomy and the physical markers of aging.</note>

->REPRODUCED IN 94 HELIOGRAPHIC PLATES<-
<note>Heliography was an early photographic process that allowed for the high-quality, permanent reproduction of images. In the 19th century, this was the gold standard for creating "facsimiles" (exact copies) of fragile historical manuscripts.</note>

->BY<-

->ANGELO DELLA CROCE<-

->MILAN — 1891<-
<note>original: "MDCCCXCI"</note>

<note>These are likely accession numbers or handwritten dates from a librarian’s cataloging process in the early 20th century.</note>

<term>Trivulzio Codex</term>
<note>A small notebook used by Leonardo da Vinci between 1487 and 1490. While it contains architectural and mechanical sketches, it is most famous for Leonardo’s "vocabulary lists"—thousands of sophisticated words he compiled to improve his literary expression and bridge the gap between his technical mind and the scholarly world of his time.</note>

<summary>This page contains the limitation statement for a rare 200-copy limited edition and the formal title page of the first scholarly publication of Leonardo's Trivulzio Codex, edited by Luca Beltrami in 1891.</summary>

<keywords>Trivulzio Codex, Leonardo da Vinci, Luca Beltrami, Milan, facsimile, heliography, character study, limited edition, 1891, Prince Trivulzio</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page is the formal dedication of the 1891 edition of the Trivulzio Codex. It follows the title page (which introduced Luca Beltrami as the editor) and precedes the main text or introduction. The dedication is directed to a fellow scholar of Leonardo da Vinci, highlighting the international academic community involved in these studies at the end of the 19th century.</meta>

## TO MR.

# DR. PAUL MÜLLER-WALDE
<note>Paul Müller-Walde (1858–1931) was a prominent German art historian and a leading Leonardo scholar of his time. His work on Leonardo’s biography and his efforts to identify lost works made him a natural peer for the editor, Luca Beltrami.</note>

### AS A SIGN OF FRIENDSHIP
<note>original: "In segno d'amicizia." This personal dedication reflects the collaborative spirit and mutual respect among late 19th-century historians who were working to systematically publish Leonardo’s dispersed manuscripts for the first time.</note>

<summary>This page contains a formal dedication from the editor, Luca Beltrami, to the German art historian Paul Müller-Walde, signaling the intellectual friendship and scholarly collaboration behind this publication.</summary>

<keywords>Paul Müller-Walde, Luca Beltrami, dedication, scholarship, art history, Leonardo da Vinci studies</keywords>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>Following the blank page 17, this page—marked as folio 1 verso—contains a diverse collection of notes ranging from historical records of the ancient world to satirical verse and brief etymological reflections.</meta>

### Table 1, folio 1 verso — (2)

Ammianus Marcellinus <note>A Roman soldier and historian of the 4th century AD, known for his history of the later Roman Empire</note> claims that seven hundred thousand volumes of books were burned in the Alexandrian conflict <note>Referring to the fire at the Library of Alexandria during Julius Caesar's siege of the city in 48–47 BCE</note> at the time of Julius Caesar.

If Petrarch loved the laurel <note>original: "ilauro"; a satirical pun on the name of Petrarch's muse, Laura, and the laurel tree</note> so intensely,
it was because it is good with sausage and thrush <note>The author mockingly suggests the poet only liked "Laura" because laurel leaves are a common seasoning for cooking meats and game birds</note>;
I cannot make a treasure of their nonsense <note>original: "giancie"; meaning idle chatter or tall tales</note>.

Two principles define being, namely ap... <unclear>ap....</unclear>

The wild <term>salvatico<gloss>savage, wild, or of the woods</gloss></term> is that which saves <note>original: "di salva"; the author is making a folk etymology or wordplay connecting "salvatico" (wild) with the verb "salvare" (to save)</note> itself.

->5<-

<summary>This page records the historical loss of the Library of Alexandria, features a satirical poem mocking Petrarch’s obsession with "Laura" by reducing her to a kitchen herb, and offers a brief philosophical or etymological note on the nature of "wildness."</summary>
<keywords>Ammianus Marcellinus, Library of Alexandria, Julius Caesar, Petrarch, Laura, satire, etymology, wildness</keywords>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>Following folio 1 verso, which contained historical anecdotes and satirical verse, this page (folio 2 recto) provides a list of books and authors belonging to Leonardo da Vinci’s personal library or reading list. These entries reveal his self-directed efforts to master subjects ranging from Latin grammar and natural science to mathematics and popular literature.</meta>

### Table 2, folio 2 recto — (3)

Donatus <note>Referring to Aelius Donatus, the 4th-century author of *Ars Minor*. This was the standard introductory Latin grammar used by students during the Renaissance; Leonardo likely used it to improve his "unlettered" status.</note>

Lapidary <note>A "Lapidario" is a treatise on the properties, virtues, and medical uses of precious stones and minerals, common in medieval and Renaissance scientific thought.</note>

Pliny <note>Gaius Plinius Secundus, or Pliny the Elder, author of the *Natural History*. This massive Roman encyclopedia was one of Leonardo's primary sources for information on the natural world.</note>

Abacus <note>An "Abacho" refers to a textbook on practical arithmetic and geometry. These books were essential for merchants, craftsmen, and engineers to learn calculation and problem-solving.</note>

Morgante <note>A celebrated epic poem by Luigi Pulci. It tells the humorous and adventurous story of a giant who converts to Christianity and follows the knight Orlando. Leonardo was known to appreciate its linguistic richness and wit.</note>

<summary>This page lists five key books from Leonardo da Vinci’s collection, documenting his diverse intellectual interests in Latin grammar, mineralogy, classical natural history, mathematics, and contemporary literature.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci's library, Donatus, Pliny the Elder, Lapidary, Abacus, Morgante, Luigi Pulci, Renaissance education</keywords>

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>Continuing from folio 2 recto, which cataloged Leonardo da Vinci’s library, this page (folio 2 verso) transitions from a list of book titles to a specific philosophical meditation attributed to Cornelius Celsus. It reflects Leonardo's practice of extracting and contemplating core ideas from the classical authors he was studying.</meta>

->Table 3<-

### Cornelius Celsus

<note>Aulus Cornelius Celsus was a Roman encyclopedist from the 1st century AD. While primarily known for his medical treatise *De Medicina*, his works often contained philosophical debates on the nature of the human condition.</note>

the supreme good <term>summum bonum</term> is wisdom. the supreme evil <term>summum malum</term> is the pain of the body. Because, as we are composed of two things, that is, of soul and of body, *the soul is superior to the body*.
Of these, the first is the better; the worse is the body. Wisdom belongs to the better part.
The supreme evil belongs to the worse part and is the worst thing. The best thing is in the soul: wisdom.
Thus, the worst thing in the body is pain. Therefore, just as the supreme evil is bodily pain, so wisdom is the supreme good of the soul—that is, of the wise man—and no other thing is to be compared to this.

<vocab>supreme good, wisdom, supreme evil, pain, body, soul, Cornelius Celsus, wise man</vocab>

<summary>In this entry, Leonardo reflects on a classical philosophical argument regarding the hierarchy of the human being, concluding that wisdom is the soul's highest good while physical pain is the body's greatest evil. This illustrates Leonardo's interest in the relationship between the physical self and the intellect.</summary>
<keywords>Cornelius Celsus, philosophy, dualism, body and soul, supreme good, wisdom, bodily pain, Renaissance thought</keywords>

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>Following the philosophical discourse on the hierarchy of soul and body on the previous page (folio 2 verso), folio 3 recto marks a transition into Leonardo's technical and scientific studies. This page serves as a formal header for a new section of his notes, pivoting from abstract classical philosophy to the practical and mathematical principles of construction and force.</meta>

# Table 4 / Folio 3 recto — (5)

->ARCHITECTURE — MECHANICS<-

<note>For Leonardo, architecture and mechanics were not separate disciplines but were linked by the laws of physics. He famously described mechanics as the "paradise of the mathematical sciences" because it was through mechanical principles that the abstract concepts of geometry and proportion found in architecture were brought to life.</note>

<vocab>architecture, mechanics, table, folio</vocab>

<summary>This page serves as a thematic section header for Leonardo's research into architecture and mechanics, signaling a shift from philosophical inquiry to the scientific and engineering challenges of the physical world.</summary>
<keywords>architecture, mechanics, engineering, Table 4, Leonardo da Vinci, scientific methodology</keywords>

--- Page 35 ---
<meta>Following the formal section header for architecture and mechanics on folio 3 recto, folio 3 verso (Table 5) presents specific scientific demonstrations. Here, Leonardo moves from general principles to specific geometric proofs regarding the behavior of light and the distribution of weight on a balance, showcasing his method of using visual diagrams to explain physical laws.</meta>

### Table 5

If **f** is a light source within a lantern, situated in the center; if the expansion created by its brightness at **c d** enters the upper dispersion <note>original: "disgregamento"; Leonardo uses this to describe the divergence or spreading out of light rays as they travel from a source</note> two times, you will also judge it to be twice as close to the light as **a b**.

->If a man jumps on the tips of his toes, the weight makes no noise<-

Every weight exerts the sum of its load directly beneath its point of resistance along a perpendicular line. <note>This refers to the "line of gravity," the vertical path along which the force of weight acts toward the center of the earth.</note> If you have a weight upon a scale which is very wide, as appears in **a b** and **c d**, and it is all joined as one, as the demonstration shows: I say that if you place 2 [units] over **c** and 3 over **d**, the greater weight will appear at scale **b**, and you will balance **a** with one pound <note>original: "libra"; a standard unit of weight</note> more than **b**.

<summary>This page contains technical notes on the inverse relationship between light dispersion and distance, alongside a study on how asymmetrical weight distribution affects the equilibrium of a balance.</summary>
<keywords>optics, light dispersion, mechanics, weight distribution, center of gravity, equilibrium, physics, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

--- Page 36 ---
<meta>Following the geometric and mechanical proofs of folio 3 verso, this page shifts focus toward natural philosophy and a specific mechanical application for courtly life. Leonardo bridges the gap between the physical laws of weights and the biological laws of the human body, viewing health as a form of equilibrium similar to the balance studied on the previous page.</meta>

### Table 6 — (7)

Medicine and the rebalancing <note>original: "riparegiamento"; a term implying the restoration of equality or balance between opposing forces</note> of unequal elements.
Illness and the discordance of elements fused within the living body. <note>Leonardo here applies the classical theory of the four elements—earth, water, air, and fire—to human health, suggesting that disease is a lack of harmony or "discordance" between these components.</note>

<note>The following lines are written toward the lower right of the page:</note>

A method of raising and lowering the curtains for the lord's silver service. <note>original: "argienti del segniore"; this likely refers to the elaborate silver plates and vessels displayed on a buffet or "credence" during a courtly banquet. Leonardo often designed theatrical and functional machinery for his patrons, such as Ludovico Sforza.</note>

<summary>This page presents a philosophical definition of medicine as the restoration of elemental balance in the body, alongside a brief note for a mechanical device used to display silverware at a noble court.</summary>
<keywords>medicine, elements, pathology, health, balance, courtly life, silverware, mechanics, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

--- Page 40 ---
<meta>Following the philosophical and mechanical notes on folio 7, this page transitions into a purely linguistic exercise. Leonardo continues his practice of list-making, likely to broaden his vocabulary for his scientific and literary writing. The page follows a phonetic organization, focusing on words starting with the "sc-" sound.</meta>

<note>This page from the Codex Trivulzianus serves as a personal dictionary or vocabulary-building exercise. Leonardo was largely self-taught in the "high" literary language of his time and often recorded lists of synonyms, related terms, or words with similar sounds to improve his expressive power. These columns specifically explore terms beginning with the "sc-" prefix, ranging from common household objects to technical, mythological, and abstract concepts.</note>

The words range from everyday items like "broom" and "monkey" to technical and philosophical terms like "scientific," "discern," and "sculpture."</summary>

--- Page 41 ---
<meta>Following the alphabetical or phonetic groupings of previous pages, this folio continues Leonardo’s "vocabulary project" in the Codex Trivulzianus. While the previous page focused on the "sc-" sound, this page moves into more abstract, legal, and philosophical terms, often grouped by Latinate prefixes (such as "pre-", "pro-", and "re-"). This demonstrates Leonardo's effort to move beyond his "unlettered" (uomo sanza lettere) roots by mastering the prestigious vocabulary of the humanist courts.</meta>

--- Page 46 ---
<meta>This page marks a transition in the Codex Trivulzianus from the preceding lists of courtly vocabulary to Leonardo’s characteristic scientific observations. While folio 5 was a linguistic exercise, folio 6 records Leonardo’s attempts to describe the physics of water—specifically how its force (impetus) changes when moving between straight lines and semicircular boundaries. The handwriting is Leonardo’s famous mirror script, written from right to left.</meta>

<note>Transcribed from Leonardo's right-to-left mirror script.</note>

->On the outer margins, the water<-

Because when the water reaches the straight line of that semicircle, it exerts within itself—the water exerts the momentum <note>original: "inpeto"; a key concept in Leonardo's physics, referring to the force or energy an object carries in motion</note>—since the water that is at the straight bottom of the semicircle, because the water that is in the straight...

The straight water appears to be the water of the semicircle point.
The water along a straight line, the water of the semicircle point.
The water which is above, the water of the semicircle point.
The water which is below, the water of the semicircle point.
The place where the water narrows <note>original: "s'assottiglia"; literally "to grow thin," describing a stream of water as it accelerates or passes through a narrower opening</note> straight throu<unclear>gh</unclear>...

### Technical Vocabulary

**water** <note>original: "acqua"</note>
**semicircle** <note>original: "mezo circhulo"</note>
**straight line** <note>original: "ricta linea"</note>
**impetus** <note>original: "inpeto"; momentum or force</note>
**straight bottom** <note>original: "dricto fondo"</note>
**it narrows** <note>original: "s'assottiglia"; thins out</note>
**outside** <note>original: "fori"</note>

<summary>A page of scientific notes where Leonardo investigates the fluid dynamics of water moving through semicircular forms, focusing on how momentum (impetus) is distributed along straight and curved lines.</summary>
<keywords>hydraulics, fluid dynamics, geometry, momentum, semicircles, physics of water, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

--- Page 47 ---
<meta>This page continues from folio 6 recto. While the previous page focused on the physical movement and momentum of water, Leonardo here pivots toward philosophical and psychological axioms. He uses the language of physics—momentum, weight, and rest—to describe the nature of love, perception, and the human intellect. This reflects his belief that the laws governing the physical world also apply to the soul and the mind.</meta>

# Plate 9. folio 6 r. — (11)

->Subject with the form<-

The lover <note>original: "lamata"; likely a variation of "l’amante," the lover</note> moves for the sake of the thing loved, just as the sense <note>the faculty of perception</note> moves toward the sensible object <note>term: "sensible object" (sensibbile); anything that can be perceived by the five senses</note>; it unites with it and they become one and the same thing.

The work <note>the result or creation</note> is the first thing born of this union. 
If the thing loved is base, the lover becomes base.

When the thing united is suited to its uniter, delight, pleasure, and satisfaction follow.

When the lover has reached the beloved, he finds rest there; 
just as when the weight is set down, it finds rest there.

The thing is known through our intellect.

### Technical Vocabulary

**subject** <note>original: "sugietto"</note>
**sensible** <note>original: "sensibbile"; that which is perceptible</note>
**is born** <note>original: "nassce"</note>
**and satisfaction** <note>original: "essadisfatione"</note>
**intellect** <note>original: "intelletto"</note>
**delight** <note>original: "dilettatione"</note>
**known** <note>original: "chogniusciuta"</note>

<summary>Leonardo presents a series of philosophical aphorisms comparing the emotional state of love and the process of sensory perception to physical laws of union and rest. He argues that the lover takes on the qualities of the beloved and that knowledge is a form of union between the intellect and the object.</summary>
<keywords>philosophy, love, perception, intellect, Aristotelianism, union, sensory experience, ethics</keywords>

--- Page 48 ---
<meta>This page continues from folio 6 recto. While the previous page dealt with the metaphysical and emotional "weight" of love and the union of the soul, Leonardo now applies these same principles of equilibrium and rest to the physical world. He transitions from the "rest" found by a lover to the "rest" found by an element within its own sphere, effectively linking his psychological theories with his studies on fluid dynamics and physics.</meta>

# Plate 10. folio 6 v. — (12)

->Table 10<-

When united,
no element <note>term: "element" (elemento); in Leonardo’s time, this referred to Earth, Water, Air, or Fire</note> will weigh within its own element; therefore, the parts of the air do not weigh upon the parts below them.

// No body of a different quality will remain still within it [the element] if it is free; because it *must be*—since it is not of the same quality as the air—either heavier or lighter. If it is heavier, it will fall downwards; if it is lighter, it will penetrate upwards toward...

That thing which has more conformity <note>similarity in density or nature</note> with the element surrounding it will exit from it with a slower motion.

And the thing which is more different will separate itself from it with greater movement and impetuous motion.
*Therefore*

And that weight, of which each of the three lighter elements <note>Water, Air, and Fire</note> [consists], will not stabilize within itself any thing outside of its nature.
*And the air demonstrates*

When force is created by motion...
When force generates a motion faster than the flight of the unresisting air, that air becomes condensed, similar to feathers pressed and trampled <note>original: "pivme premvte e chalchate"</note> [under] the <unclear>certain</unclear> weight of a sleeping man. And that thing which was driving the air, finding resistance in it, bounces back like a ball struck against a wall. <note>This is a foundational observation for Leonardo’s studies on flight: the idea that air can provide "solid" resistance if struck quickly enough.</note>

### A way to see the eclipsed sun without suffering to the eye

Take a sheet of paper and make holes in it with a needle; and through those holes, look at the sun.
A way to see the eclipsed sun without suffering <note>original: "passione"; meaning physical pain or damage to the retina</note> to the eye.

### Technical Vocabulary

**element** <note>original: "elemento"</note>
**air** <note>original: "aria"</note>
**body** <note>original: "chorpo"</note>
**weight** <note>original: "peso"</note>
**motion** <note>original: "moto"</note>
**eclipsed sun** <note>original: "sole eclissato"</note>

<summary>Leonardo explores the physics of buoyancy and air resistance, suggesting that air "condenses" under rapid force like a compressed cushion, which provides the resistance necessary for flight. He also records a practical method for observing a solar eclipse using a pinhole effect to protect the eyes.</summary>
<keywords>fluid dynamics, buoyancy, air compression, aerodynamics, solar eclipse, pinhole camera, elements, physics of motion</keywords>

--- Page 49 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the physical equilibrium of elements discussed on folio 6 verso to the mechanics of how motion, light, and sound are transmitted through space. Leonardo applies his understanding of geometric proportions to the senses, moving from the "rest" of the soul to the active "soul" as a prime mover of the body.</meta>

# Plate 45. folio 45 r.

To refresh <note>original: "di rinfrescare"; likely a fragmented note or a reminder for a specific artistic or scientific task.</note>

Nothing moves by itself unless it is moved by another; and if it is moved, it is by cause of the soul <note>term: "soul" (anima); in this context, Leonardo refers to the internal animating principle or biological motor that initiates movement in living beings.</note> moved by itself.

Every thing that sends itself forth by rays—whether of voice, or of smell, or of sight—sends its likeness <note>term: "likeness" (similitudine); Leonardo believed that objects emit invisible physical "species" or images of themselves that travel through the air to the senses.</note> through the air to the eyes, or the smell to the noses, or the voice to the ears. The voice sends itself through the air in circular waves, just as a stone falling into water sends its circles through the water.

The further the motion is removed from its cause, the more that motion must diminish.
The motion of sight is sent through the air with pyramidal angles. <note>Leonardo’s "pyramid of sight" theory: he envisioned light and vision as cones or pyramids with the base at the object and the apex at the eye.</note>

And of the rays of the sun that strike within the angles of the rays of motions.

<margin>
- the cause of the
pyramidal motions
</margin>

->**Of the eye and of the voice**<-

Every body acts through pyramidal rays upon the air, and those rays which are more obtuse <note>wider or less sharp</note> have more force, and those which are more acute <note>sharper/narrower</note> have less force; and this arises because the air receives within itself all the likenesses of bodies sent by them in acute angles, and the further they go, the less of them is seen.

<note>Diagram illustrating optical projection: a circular source (likely the sun or eye) on the left emits rays that pass through a narrow aperture in a vertical screen, demonstrating the pyramidal nature of light propagation.</note>

Every body gives [itself] through pyramidal rays upon the air, and those rays which are more obtuse have less force, and those rays which are more acute have less force; <note>Leonardo repeats and slightly varies this observation, likely refining his thought on how light intensity or "force" dissipates over distance.</note> and this arises because the air receives within itself all the likenesses of bodies.

A way to see the eclipsed sun without harm to the eye. <note>This refers to the "camera obscura" principle, where sunlight passing through a small hole projects an image onto a surface, allowing one to observe an eclipse safely.</note>

<summary>Leonardo explores the physics of sensory perception, arguing that light, sound, and smell travel through the air in waves or pyramidal forms. He specifically notes a safe method for observing solar eclipses using the principles of optical projection.</summary>
<keywords>optics, acoustics, soul as prime mover, pyramidal rays, likenesses, solar eclipse, camera obscura, wave propagation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the physics of motion. While the previous page focused on how light, sound, and sight travel through space via "pyramidal rays," this entry moves to the fundamental relationship between a physical object and the force (the "mover") that initiates its displacement.</meta>

# Plate II.a folio 7 r. — (13)

->When the moved thing is united with its mover<- <note>This sentence reflects a principle of Aristotelian physics often cited by Leonardo: for an object to move, it must be in direct contact or "united" with the force—the mover—acting upon it.</note>

**the thing**, **moved**, **united**, **mover** <note>original: "lachosa, mossa, vnita, motore"; these terms form the core vocabulary for Leonardo's study of mechanics and dynamics.</note>

<summary>This page presents a foundational axiom of Renaissance mechanics concerning the direct physical contact required between a moving object and its source of motion.</summary>
<keywords>motion, mover, physics, mechanics, force, unity</keywords>

--- Page 55 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the general mechanics of motion discussed on the previous page to the specific physics of elements—specifically the relationship between Earth and Water. Leonardo is applying his "pyramidal" or proportional logic to hydrostatics and the center of gravity of the globe.</meta>

# f. 14r
## 51

The highest remedy <note>original: "sommo di rimedjo"; in this context, it refers to the definitive solution or principle</note> shall be this, which must be done when the weight <note>original: "boja"; likely an idiosyncratic spelling for a buoy or a specific mechanical weight used in his experiments</note> departs
<margin>ing</margin> <note>original: "a"; a fragment completing the word "parting"</note>
->--- departing ---<-

+ By as much as the radius of the earth is greater than that of the water, by so much is the water heavier in the heavens <note>original: "nel cielo"; referring to the atmosphere or the space surrounding the elemental spheres</note>
<margin>than</margin> <note>original: "on"; likely a fragment of "non" or a connective used in the comparison</note>
--- than the earth: and the reason shall be this, because the center of gravity
->of the earth<-
shall be lower than the center of the sphere of water: the reason being

**Technical Terms:**
<term>remedy</term> <note>rimedjo: the corrective measure or scientific principle</note>, <term>weight</term> <note>boja: a weight or buoy used in displacement</note>, <term>reason</term> <note>raggjone: the logical cause or proportion</note>, <term>gravity</term> <note>gravezza: heaviness or the downward force of an element</note>, <term>lower</term> <note>baxa: situated further toward the center</note>, <term>sphere</term> <note>spera: the natural circular domain of an element</note>, <term>radius</term> <note>raggio: the distance from the center to the surface</note>

<summary>Leonardo explores the relative weight and centers of gravity of Earth and Water, positing that the difference in their radii affects how heavy they are perceived to be in relation to the center of the world.</summary>
<keywords>gravity, hydrostatics, earth, water, radius, center of gravity, physics of elements, Codex Forster III</keywords>

--- Page 58 ---
<meta>This page shifts from the theoretical discussion of gravity and elemental spheres to a practical engineering application: the structural stability of the tiburio (crossing tower) for Milan Cathedral. Leonardo applies his understanding of forces—specifically the outward thrust of arches—to prevent the collapse of large masonry structures.</meta>

# f. 15r
## 8

The way to give strength to the crossing tower <note>original: "tivurio"; the polygonal structure covering the dome at the intersection of a cathedral's nave and transept</note>
because the arch, by its weight <note>original: "gravezza"; the downward force of the masonry</note>, pushes 
outward at the haunches <note>original: "reni"; literally 'kidneys', the 'shoulders' or middle sections of an arch where the lateral pressure is strongest</note> and causes the ruin of the crossing tower;
wherefore it is necessary to create the chain <note>original: "catena"; a metal tie-rod or tension member used to bind the structure</note> that holds it,
and it will go on restraining, restraining. <note>original: "r[at]enendo"; referring to the continuous counter-force provided by the tension of the chain</note>

And this is our architecture.

<margin>
<note>Extremely faded note</note>
<unclear>arch</unclear>
</margin>

**Technical Terms:**
<term>crossing tower</term> <note>tivurio: the tower or lantern typical of Lombard architecture that encloses a dome</note>, <term>weight</term> <note>gravezza: the gravity or heaviness of the material that creates structural pressure</note>, <term>haunches</term> <note>reni: the points of an arch situated between the base and the peak where the structure is most prone to pushing outward</note>, <term>chain</term> <note>catena: an engineering solution using iron or wood to encircle a dome or arch to counteract lateral thrust</note>, <term>ruin</term> <note>roina: the structural failure or collapse of a building</note>

<summary>Leonardo analyzes the structural mechanics of the Milan Cathedral’s crossing tower, identifying how the weight of arches creates outward thrust that must be countered with tension chains to prevent collapse.</summary>
<keywords>architecture, engineering, Milan Cathedral, tiburio, arches, structural thrust, tension chains, Renaissance construction</keywords>

--- Page 59 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s investigation into the structural integrity of large buildings, moving from the specific problem of the Milan Cathedral’s crossing tower to a more general principle of architectural engineering: the use of the inverted arch.</meta>

### Plate 13, folio 8 recto — (15)

<margin><note>original: "auida"</note> Eager</margin>

The reversed arch <note>original: "larcho riuerscio"; an inverted or U-shaped arch used to distribute weight across a wider base</note> is better for providing a shoulder <note>original: "isspalla"; a structural support or buttress that resists lateral thrust</note> than the ordinary arch, because the reversed arch finds beneath itself a wall resistant to its weakness <note>the 'weakness' refers to the point in an arch where structural failure is most likely under pressure</note>, while the ordinary arch finds nothing but air at its weak point.

Architecture noted. <note>original: "archittetura notta"; a concluding remark indicating a finalized observation or a principle to be remembered</note>

**Technical Terms:**
<term>reversed arch</term> <note>archo riuerscio: an inverted arch used in foundations or between piers to redirect downward pressure and prevent the walls from spreading or sinking</note>, <term>shoulder</term> <note>isspalla: the flanking part of a structure that provides lateral support; a buttress</note>, <term>resistant wall</term> <note>muro resistente: a solid masonry element capable of withstanding the forces exerted by an arch</note>, <term>architecture noted</term> <note>archittetura notta: a phrase used by Leonardo to categorize this entry as a formal architectural rule or observation</note>

<summary>Leonardo argues that inverted arches are superior to standard arches for buttressing because their most vulnerable points are supported by solid masonry rather than open air.</summary>
<keywords>structural engineering, reversed arch, buttressing, masonry, architectural theory, mechanics, lateral thrust</keywords>

### Plate 16 folio 10 v. — (20)

to lacerate <note>original: "stratiare"; often used by Leonardo to describe the physical tearing of flesh in anatomical or combat descriptions.</note>

illustrious <note>original: "inclito"; a high-style poetic term for someone renowned.</note>

The eye that finds itself positioned between the shadows and the surrounding lights of shaded bodies will see upon those bodies the greatest shadows that can be found there, meeting the eye at equal angles—that is, the angles of <term>visual incidence</term>. <note>Visual incidence refers to the perspective law where the angle at which a ray of light (or the line of sight) hits a surface determines how the viewer perceives the intensity of light and shadow.</note>

# Folio 22r

<note>At the top left, a sketchy landscape study depicts a rugged mountain or cliff with structures, possibly a castle or town, at its base.</note>

Every thing gathers in its place, or in moving toward it, just as water flows to the sea; because of which, the air <note>Leonardo is describing the Aristotelian concept of "natural place," suggesting that all elements possess an inherent tendency to return to their point of origin—water to the ocean and air to the sky.</note>

---

<note>Leonardo discusses the geometry of light and shadow, comparing pyramids of illumination and darkness.</note>

The pyramid of shadow is similar to that of light. The pyramid of shadow has for its base the base of the light, and the point of the shadow is similar to the base of the pyramid of light. <note>In Leonardo’s optical theory, light and shadow are treated as geometric "pyramids" (cones) that mirror one another. The "base" refers to the widest part of the light source or the object casting the shadow.</note>

of shadow

the pyramid
of light
</margin>

<note>A diagram of a pyramid or cone divided by horizontal parallel lines accompanies this text, illustrating the convergence of shadow and light.</note>

<note>Leonardo da Vinci was largely "omo sanza lettere" (a man without letters), meaning he lacked a formal classical education in Latin. In his middle age, he began compiling long lists of words—thousands of them—to improve his vocabulary and master the "illustrious" Tuscan dialect used by the great poets and scholars of his time.</note>

Leonardo’s notebooks were often composed of loose sheets bound together later, which explains why the alphabetical sequence does not follow the page numbers. These lists show Leonardo "studying" language, attempting to incorporate Latinate and sophisticated vocabulary into his repertoire to better communicate his scientific and philosophical ideas.</meta>

<meta>This page transitions from the alphabetical vocabulary lists seen in the earlier folios (such as 13v) to a more advanced rhetorical exercise. On Folio 81r of the Codex Trivulziano, Leonardo is no longer just defining words; he is practicing "permutations"—rearranging sets of high-minded, Latinate concepts to explore their rhythmic and logical variations. This reflects his effort to transition from an "unlettered man" (uomo sanza lettere) to a sophisticated communicator of philosophy and science.</meta>

<note>This page is organized into two distinct columns of word lists. Leonardo is experimenting with how different abstract virtues and concepts—spirit, soul, wisdom, justice, friendship, and peace—interact and flow together in various sequences.</note>

<meta>The previous page (folio 13v) focused on simple vocabulary definitions. This page marks a shift in Leonardo da Vinci’s self-education. Here, on a divider page likely prepared by a later editor (Ravaisson-Mollien) for folio 28 verso, we see Leonardo’s "permutations." He is no longer just defining words; he is practicing how to combine high-minded concepts like "spirit," "justice," and "wisdom" into rhythmic, rhetorical patterns. This reflects his ambition to master the "mother tongue" and the formal language of philosophers.</meta>

fol. 29 r. — (28)

# Plate 24

Every man always finds himself in the middle of the world, beneath the middle of his <term>hemisphere</term><note>original: "emisperio." In Leonardo’s time, this referred to the visible dome of the sky above an observer.</note>, and above the center of that world.

---

->the straighter the line, the more it resists<-
<note>This is a principle of mechanics; a straight beam or line can withstand more force or tension than a curved one before deforming.</note>

---

->sky &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; candle<-

---

<margin>
The <term>primitive shadow</term> and <term>derivative shadow</term> will be greater when caused by the light of a candle than by the light of the sky.
<note>In Leonardo’s theory of optics, the "primitive shadow" is the darkness on the part of an object not reached by light. The "derivative shadow" is the shadow cast by that object onto a separate surface or through the air. He is noting that a point-source of light (the candle) creates more distinct and larger shadows than a diffuse source (the sky).</note>
</margin>

<margin>
Just as much as the larger <term>derivative shadow</term> enters into the smaller, so much is the cause of the smaller more luminous than the larger.
<note>Leonardo is analyzing the relationship between the intensity of a light source and the density of the shadow it casts. He observes that a brighter, more concentrated light source results in a sharper, "smaller" core shadow compared to a larger, dimmer source.</note>
</margin>

<summary>Leonardo reflects on the observer's central position in the universe and explores the relationship between light sources—specifically the sky versus a candle—and the size and intensity of the shadows they produce.</summary>
<keywords>optics, primitive shadow, derivative shadow, mechanics, light sources, perspective, geometry</keywords>

# Plate 25

Just as much as **a b** enters into **c d**, so much will **a n** be darker than **e r**.
<note>Leonardo is using alphabetical labels to refer to a diagram (not present in the text) illustrating the proportional relationship between the intensity of light and the depth of shadow.</note>

---

The edges of the window, being illuminated by two different lights of different brightness, will cast light into the dwelling of different qualities.
<note>Leonardo is observing how the "jambs" or inner surfaces of a window frame reflect light into a room. The "quality" of light depends on whether the source is the bright sun, a cloudy sky, or an artificial flame.</note>

---

If **b** is a candle and **a c** is our <term>hemisphere</term><note>original: "emisperio." Leonardo uses this term to describe the entire visible dome of the sky as a single, massive light source.</note>, both illuminate the edges of the window **m n**; but the light **b** only illuminates **f g**, while the hemisphere **a c** will illuminate as far as **d e**.
<note>This describes a geometric principle: a small, nearby light source (the candle) has a limited angle of reach, whereas a large, distant source (the sky) can illuminate much further into the interior corners of a window opening.</note>

<summary>Leonardo analyzes how the size and distance of a light source—comparing a candle to the open sky—affect how light penetrates a window and illuminates a room's interior.</summary>
<keywords>optics, light sources, illumination, window, geometry, hemisphere, shadows</keywords>

# Plate 28
## folio 27 r. — (32)

->just as a day well spent brings happy sleep<-
->so a life well used brings happy death<-
->**note**<-

<note>original: "sichome . vna . giornata . bene spesa dallieto . dormire / cosi . vna vita . bene . vsata . da lieto . morire". This is one of Leonardo's most celebrated aphorisms. It reflects his "workmanlike" view of existence: he believed that nature rewards effort and that death is not to be feared if one has fulfilled their purpose. To Leonardo, "well used" meant a life dedicated to observation, creation, and the pursuit of knowledge.</note>

<vocab>just as, one, day, used, to die, note</vocab>

# Plate 29
## folio 26 v.

This is the method by which the molds <term>forme: hollow casting molds, likely made of clay or plaster, used for creating sculptures or metal objects</term> are dried with speed as they are continually turned, just like roasts <note>original: "arosti." ## Plate 30

where it will stop
That thing which, within the line of equality <term>linia dellequalita: likely referring to a horizontal level or a state of equilibrium</term>, is found further from its support <term>fermamento: a fixed point, anchor, or fulcrum</term>, will be less supported by it, as is shown in *m* and *n* below.

where and why
it will stop
<column-break/>
### Every
That thing which is further from its support will be less supported by it; whereby, with more freedom, it will follow its natural desire <note>In Renaissance physics, "natural desire" refers to the inherent tendency of heavy objects to move toward the center of the Earth.</note>.

Violent motion <term>moto violente: motion produced by an external force, such as a throw or a machine</term>, the more it is exercised, the more it weakens.
Natural motion <note>Leonardo refers to gravity-driven motion here.</note> does the opposite <note>original: "leposito" (the opposite). Leonardo observed that while a thrown object slows down, a falling object accelerates.</note>.
<column-break/>
freely
it obeys more

That thing which is more distant from its support will be less supported by it; being less supported, it will participate more in its freedom; and because a free weight always descends, this thing—being heavy—will therefore descend more quickly.
<column-break/>
That extremity of the rod <term>aste: a pole, beam, or lever arm</term> which is further from its support will be less supported by that support; wherefore, being less supported, it more freely retains the ability to follow its nature; its nature being heavy, and it being the nature of heavy things to desire to descend, this part will therefore descend more quickly than any other part.

<summary>Leonardo examines the principles of leverage and gravity, arguing that the further a weight is from its support, the "freer" it is to follow its natural tendency to fall.</summary>
<keywords>mechanics, gravity, equilibrium, natural motion, violent motion, levers, Aristotelian physics, Renaissance science</keywords>

--- Page 109 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo's exploration of theoretical mechanics, moving from the levers and natural descent discussed on the previous page to the behavior of tension in cords and pulleys. He introduces the concept of "rinterro di forza" (the unification or distribution of force), analyzing how tension is distributed equally throughout a cord when balanced by weights.</meta>

If a cord is pulled by two equal
forces of equal weight, it will be in equilibrium. The
cord will be unified; within it, every
part will be equally strained.

<note>Study of a single cord pulled by equal forces at both ends.</note>

---

<margin>
in such a way
that it remains
equal
</margin>

If a cord supports two equal
forces of equal weight and is
passed over a pulley wheel <term>girigola: a pulley or small wheel used to redirect a cord</term>,
this is called the distribution of force <term>rinterro di forza: literally the "making whole" of force; a state where tension is balanced and unified across a mechanical system</term>.

<note>Mechanical study of a pulley with two equal weights.</note>

---

The distribution of force is achieved in two ways: either
by equality of force or by equality of weight;
this is proven.

---

If a force is equal to a weight,
and is pulled by the said force
in such a way that there is a distribution of
force—either by a pulley <term>rotola: a roller or small pulley wheel</term>, by a rim <term>ciglio: literally an "edge" or "lip," here referring to the grooved rim of a wheel</term>,
or by a binding—the said force
will not be strained to pull
one of the weights, but will be strained
by the pulling of both weights;
and if the said force is pulled
from one of the sides, it will be moved.

Whether the said force is equal
to the distribution of the force, or whether
it is achieved by a rim, or by a pulley,
or by a binding, the force
of the motion of the force
that pulls the said force will be distributed,
because the said force... <note>Leonardo's sentence remains incomplete, but he is arguing that in a balanced system, a pulling force must overcome the combined resistance of the weights.</note>

<summary>Leonardo defines the mechanical principles of equilibrium in pulley systems, explaining how tension is distributed equally throughout a cord when balanced by identical weights.</summary>

<meta>Continuing from the previous page of the Codex Trivulzianus, which focused on words beginning with the prefix "ri-," this page shows Leonardo shifting his attention to other prefixes and letters. He is expanding his vocabulary through a process of "vernacularization"—taking Latinate or "learned" terms and incorporating them into his own Italian writing. This page is particularly dense with terms related to law, philosophy, and the character of actions, organized into five neat columns despite being written in his famous mirror script.</meta>

<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s systematic linguistic exercises. While the previous folio focused on nouns related to philosophy and physics, this page (f. 39r) is almost entirely dedicated to a rhythmic categorization of verbs. These are organized by prefixes—primarily those starting with "a-", "con-", "in-", "pro-", and "dis-". This reflects Leonardo's effort to expand his literary vocabulary, moving from the simple vernacular of his youth toward the "high" style of humanistic Italian and Latin-influenced terminology.</meta>

Folio 22 r. — (42)
Plate 38

## ARCHITECTURE

<term>architecture<note>The Italian term "architettura" in Leonardo's context refers to the "science of building." He viewed it as a discipline fundamentally rooted in geometry and mechanics, particularly the study of how forces move through stone and timber.</note></term>

<keywords>architecture, structural design, title page, building science</keywords>

The transcription reflects the fragmented nature of his notebooks, where small mechanical observations are often tucked into the margins of larger architectural studies. This particular fragment discusses a mechanism for automated door closure.</meta>

### Folio 45

**Plate 41**

; everything is understood by its <term>downward motion<note>Original Italian: "moto di su in giù." In the context of Leonardo’s mechanical studies, this refers to the way gravity acts upon objects. By observing how things "want" to fall, an architect can calculate the necessary supports to keep a building—like a dome—standing.</note></term>

<note>This single line is written in Leonardo’s characteristic mirror script, reading from right to left. The transcription reflects his use of nasal bars (tildes) over vowels as a shorthand for the letter 'n', a common practice in Renaissance writing.</note>

<summary>A single-sentence philosophical and mechanical premise stating that the nature of all things is revealed through their downward movement or gravitational pull.</summary>
<keywords>mechanics, gravity, motion, architectural theory, Leonardo da Vinci, mirror script</keywords>

--- Page 143 ---
<meta>This page serves as a philosophical bridge following the mechanical observations on Folio 44. While the previous folio focused on the physical laws of gravity and architecture, this page establishes the epistemological foundation for all of Leonardo's work. The page is marked with the number "139" in the top right corner, and the ink from the reverse side has bled through slightly, creating a "ghosting" effect common in Renaissance manuscripts.</meta>

### Folio 45

**Plate 41, Folio 20 verso — (45)**

->All our <term>knowledge<note>Original: "cognitione." In Leonardo's usage, this implies the process of acquiring certainty through the intellect and experience combined.</note></term> <term>begins<note>Original: "prencipia." This indicates a point of origin or a foundational principle.</note></term> with the <term>senses<note>Original: "sentimenti." In the 15th century, this term referred to sensory perceptions—sight, hearing, touch, etc.—rather than modern "emotions." This is a cornerstone of Leonardo's empirical method: truth is found through observation of the world.</note></term><-

<note>This single, centered line is a variation of the Aristotelian and Scholastic maxim *Nihil est in intellectu quod non prius fuerit in sensu* (Nothing is in the intellect that was not first in the senses). Leonardo lived by this rule, prioritizing his eyes and hands over the theoretical debates of ancient books.</note>

<summary>A foundational philosophical aphorism stating that all human understanding is derived from sensory experience and empirical observation.</summary>
<keywords>epistemology, empiricism, sensory perception, philosophy of science, Leonardo da Vinci, observation</keywords>

--- Page 145 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s investigation into the physics of flight, moving from the philosophical origins of knowledge on the previous page to the mechanical reality of how a bird sustains itself in the air. While the previous page (Folio 45) established that all knowledge begins with the senses, this folio (97r) applies that observation to the fluid dynamics of air and the anatomy of wings.</meta>

### Folio 97 Recto

**Plate 41, Folio 20 recto — (97)**

Air can be compressed more than water, and when the movements that drive it are faster than the escape of said air, that part which is closest to the <term>mover<note>Original: "motore." In Renaissance mechanics, the 'motor' or 'mover' refers to the source of power or the object exerting force—in this case, the bird's wing.</note></term> becomes denser, whereby it offers greater resistance; and when the motion made within it is faster than the escape of that air, the primary mover will be forced to make a contrary movement, as <unclear>occurs</unclear> in <unclear>birds</unclear>.

Since they are unable to drive the tips of their wings downward with the [full] <term>velocity<note>Original: "velocità." Leonardo uses this to describe the rapid displacement of the wing against the air's resistance.</note></term> with which they are moved—because the force of the primary mover drives them—it follows that the bird lifts itself upward by as much as the extremity of the wing is prevented from moving downward.

This is similar to a man who holds his hands and chest near a <term>wall<note>Original: "muro." This famous analogy illustrates the principle of action and reaction: if the medium (the air or the wall) does not give way, the force is redirected back to the object (the man or the bird).</note></term> and exerts force with his hands against that wall; if the wall does not give way, the man must necessarily move backward.

<summary>Leonardo explains the mechanics of flight through air resistance, arguing that a bird rises because the air beneath its wings becomes compressed and dense, acting like a solid wall that pushes the bird upward.</summary>
<keywords>aerodynamics, fluid dynamics, flight, bird anatomy, resistance, compression, physics of motion, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

--- Page 148 ---
<meta>While the previous page focused on the external physics of flight and the resistance of air, Leonardo here turns his attention inward to the physiological and psychological responses of the human body. He transitions from how air affects a bird's wing to how sound and fear affect the human "animo" (the mind or animating spirit) and the nervous system, continuing his holistic study of how forces—both physical and sensory—impact living beings.</meta>

->The whole body<-

---

The screeching of a craftsman’s saw sets the <term>teeth on edge<note>Original: "denti lunghi," literally "long teeth." This is a traditional Italian idiom for the unpleasant, tingling sensation in the teeth caused by harsh sounds.</note></term> for those who hear it. This happens because the mind imagines that screeching to be so great that it would cause significant pain to whoever produced it. Therefore, for this reason, the nerves of the voice and those of the teeth are moved; through such commotion, the mind believes itself to be under attack. From this arises that tremor. This trembling is born of nothing other than a chilled mind, which, out of fear, has <term>shriveled<note>Original: "raggricchiato." Leonardo uses this to describe the physical contraction or "curling up" of the spirit when it retreats from a perceived threat.</note></term> into itself and can no longer control the limbs. The limbs are then shaken by the mind for fear of perishing.

<note>Small diagram of a radiant circle, likely representing a point of sensory impact or the propagation of a sensation like sound or fear.</note>

Furthermore, the fear of boiling water, or of something about to be touched, causes the teeth to tremble no less than the aforementioned screeching does. This arises because the mind does not wish to see the resulting effect. The mind is shaken within itself by such fear, and as it shrivels up, it cannot help but shake the limbs against its own <term>will<note>Original: "uoglia." Leonardo suggests here that the physical tremor is an involuntary reaction of the "animo" that bypasses conscious control.</note></term>, which is not to blame.

<vocab>screeching (stridore), saw (segha), teeth on edge (denti lunghi), shriveled (raggricchiato), tremor (tremore), limbs (membra), mind/soul (animo)</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo examines the physiological reaction to harsh sounds and fear, concluding that the mind's attempt to protect the body causes involuntary tremors and the sensation of teeth being "set on edge."</summary>
<keywords>physiology, acoustics, nervous system, fear, involuntary motion, Leonardo da Vinci, sensory perception</keywords>

--- Page 149 ---
<meta>This page shifts from the previous folio's exploration of human physiology—fear and the nervous system—to practical military technology. Leonardo transitions from how the mind reacts to external stimuli to the creation of external "stimuli" for warfare, specifically focusing on incendiary devices and naval defense tactics.</meta>

# Plate 43.

->Greek Fire<-

Take willow charcoal, <term>saltpeter<note>Original: "salenitro." Potassium nitrate, a key ingredient in gunpowder and incendiaries.</note></term>, brandy, and sulfur, pitch with incense, camphor, and <term>Ethiopian wool<note>Original: "lana etiopicha." This likely refers to a type of fibrous material used as a wick; in some medieval contexts, it was a name for asbestos, but here it is intended to be combustible.</note></term>. Boil everything together. This fire has such a desire to burn that it follows the wood even under water.

And if you add to this composition <term>liquid varnish<note>Original: "vèrnice liquida." Often a mixture of resins and linseed oil.</note></term>, petroleum oil, turpentine, and strong vinegar, mix everything together and dry it in the sun, or in an oven after the bread has been removed. Then wrap it around <term>hemp tow<note>Original: "stopa di canapa." The coarse, fibrous remains of hemp used for stuffing or wicking.</note></term> or other fibers, shaping it into a round ball. Drive very sharp nails into it from every side; only leave a hole in said ball for a <term>vent<note>Original: "raza." Refers to an opening or fuse-hole to allow the fire to escape or be ignited.</note></term>. Then cover it with <term>colophony<note>Original: "colofonia." A translucent resin obtained from pines, used here to seal the device and add fuel.</note></term> and sulfur.

Furthermore, this fire can be attached to the end of a long pole, which should have one <term>cubit<note>Original: "1º br." (one braccio). A Florentine unit of measurement approximately 58 centimeters or 23 inches.</note></term> of iron at the tip so that it is not burned by said fire. This is useful for repelling and preventing enemy ships, so as not to be overwhelmed by their onset.

Also, throw glass jars full of pitch onto the enemy vessels while the men on them are intent on the battle; then, by throwing similar balls [of fire] afterward, they increase the power to burn every ship.

<summary>Leonardo describes a recipe for "Greek Fire," a potent incendiary substance, and details the construction of spiked fireballs and naval defense tactics using long poles and glass jars of pitch.</summary>
<keywords>Greek fire, incendiary weapons, naval warfare, saltpeter, petroleum oil, military engineering, naval tactics</keywords>

--- Page 150 ---
<meta>This page follows Leonardo’s discussion of military technology and incendiaries. It transitions from the chemical composition of 'Greek Fire' on the previous page to maritime survival—specifically how to treat seasickness and obtain fresh water at sea—before launching into a detailed physical analysis of the acoustics and concussive force of artillery fire.</meta>

# Plate 44.

Those who are troubled by vomiting while sailing should drink **juice of wormwood**.

Seawater filtered through mud or clay leaves all its saltiness behind.

Woolen cloths woven and hung from the sides of the ship absorb fresh water.

If you distill seawater with a <term>bell jar<note>Original: "canpana." A bell-shaped vessel used in early distillation setups.</note></term>, it will be of the highest quality; and by adapting a small furnace to his galley, the same wood used for cooking will distill a great quantity of water if the bell jar is large.

### The nature of the effect of the bombard's thunder

The noise of <term>bombards<note>Original: "bonbarde." Large-caliber medieval cannons used for sieges.</note></term> is caused by the impetuous fury of the flame striking back against the resisting air. This effect occurs because of the quantity of powder that finds itself ignited within the body of the bombard; not finding itself in a place large enough for its expansion, nature directs it to seek with fury a place capable of receiving its growth. Breaking or clearing away the weakest obstacle, it reaches the open air, which does not yield with the same speed at which it is attacked. Because fire is more <term>subtle<note>Original: "sottile." In Leonardo's physics, a "subtle" substance is finer, less dense, and faster-moving than a "gross" or thick substance.</note></term> than air, it follows that since the air is not of equal subtlety, it cannot give way with the speed and swiftness of the fire without the air resisting. This resistance is the cause of the great crash and noise of the bombard.

But if the bombard were fired against the coming of an impetuous wind, you would hear a louder thunder, caused by the reason of the greater resistance of the air against the flame; and likewise, it would make less noise when fired along the line of the wind, because there would be less resistance. In marshy places or other areas with thick air, the bombard will make a greater noise nearby, but it will be heard less far away than on mountains or other places with thin air.

If the air is thick or thin equally, and without the movement of winds, the noise will be heard equally all around its cause, spreading from circle to circle, similar to the circles made in water caused by a stone thrown into it. And in the place where such instruments are fired, the nearby air will break all structures or move all things of weak resistance. All large vessels with wide mouths will be broken, as well as paper windows and similar things. All nearby roofs will be moved from their supports, and this will happen when many windows and doors are open; it will also bring danger to thin and <term>unarmed walls<note>Original: "disarmati mvri." Walls lacking structural reinforcement or thickness.</note></term>. This happens because the air swells and presses and resounds in every direction. Regarding the movement: doors, windows, trees, and similar things will be moved. And if you were to place an arrow loosely with a small stone on top, even at a distance of 6 miles, it would fall due to the movement of the air.

<summary>Leonardo describes methods for desalting water and curing seasickness before analyzing the physics of cannon blasts, specifically how air resistance creates sound and shockwaves.</summary>
<keywords>seasickness, distillation, seawater, bombard, acoustics, air resistance, shockwaves, ballistics</keywords>

--- Page 151 ---
<meta>Continuity with page 146: Leonardo transitions from the physical mechanics of artillery and maritime survival to a metaphysical and physical meditation on the nature of the soul and the concept of "force" (la forza). While the previous page dealt with the "fury" of gunpowder, this page explores "fury" as a fundamental property of physics and biology.</meta>

Every part has an inclination to reconnect itself to its whole in order to escape its own imperfection.

The soul desires to remain with its body, because without the organic instruments of that body, it can neither act nor feel anything.

You see that the hope and the desire to return to one's homeland and to return to the original state is inherent to the human species, in the same way as the moth <note>original: "farfalla." This can mean butterfly or moth; given the context of "to the light," it refers to a moth’s fatal attraction to a flame.</note> is drawn to the light. And the man who, with perpetual longing and joyful anticipation, always awaits the new spring, always the new summer, always the new months and the new years—feeling that the things he desires are too slow in coming—does not realize that he is actually desiring his own destruction. But this desire exists in that <unclear>quintessence</unclear> <note>original: "quinta essentia." In medieval science, the fifth element beyond earth, air, fire, and water, thought to compose the celestial bodies.</note>, the spirit of the elements, which, finding itself enclosed within the soul of the human body, always desires to return to its sender. And I want you to know that this same desire is that <unclear>quintessence</unclear>, the companion of nature; and man is a **model of the world** <note>original: "modello dello mondo." Referring to the concept of the Microcosm, where the human body reflects the structure and laws of the entire Universe.</note>.

Force is nothing other than a spiritual virtue, an invisible power which, through accidental external violence, is caused by motion and is enclosed and infused within members <note>meaning limbs or mechanical parts</note> that are drawn and bent away from their natural use and life. The shorter the life of this force, the greater its power, and it always consumes itself through violence. If it is great, it possesses great fury, and it drives itself out by its own strength, wishing to overcome the cause of its violence. It conquers all things and always desires to flee its opposite, and while dying it grows, and willingly consumes the cause of its life. It conquers and casts down all opposition; it conquers everything; it desires its own destruction, driving out what opposes it and wishing to kill everything, including itself. Unhappy thing, it is always changing, and the more it grows, the more it is consumed. It <unclear>purges</unclear> itself; by force it drives out what contrasts it; it willingly desires to flee its cause and is always failing until it has returned to its natural veins. And in every motion it changes. Nothing moves without

<vocab>soul, body, imperfection, hope, desire, quintessence, sender, model of the world (microcosm), force, spiritual virtue, invisible power, violence</vocab>

<summary>A profound philosophical meditation where Leonardo describes the human desire for time to pass as a subconscious yearning for the soul to return to its source, and defines "force" as an invisible, violent power that seeks its own destruction.</summary>
<keywords>soul and body, quintessence, microcosm, physics of force, spiritual virtue, nature of desire, Leonardo da Vinci philosophy</keywords>

<meta>Continuity with page 146: On the previous page, Leonardo explored the metaphysical nature of "force" and the soul's desire to return to its origin, comparing man to a "model of the world" (microcosm). # Page 53

<note>This page contains Leonardo's mirror writing and several mechanical sketches related to the dynamics of water wheels and mill machinery. The text is written in Leonardo's characteristic right-to-left mirror script, transcribed here in standard left-to-right orientation.</note>

<margin>
<note>Top right annotation next to a hollow cylinder drawing</note>
how this large wheel 
should wear down <note>original: "chōsuma," meaning to consume or wear away through use/friction.</note>
</margin>

<note>Mechanical drawing of a horizontal axle with a wheel and a long cylindrical drum</note>

<margin>
<note>Text immediately below the axle drawing</note>
how a machine <term>ordegno</term> <note>A general term Leonardo uses for a mechanical device, engine, or apparatus.</note> wears down by reason of the weights
</margin>

No water wheel in a mill, among those moved by the water of a river, will ever have so much force that—if it were stationary—it would not have to increase in force because of the water that strikes it, by reason of the weight of the water that is loaded into it. This is true if it were round like **r. s.** and the water fell upon it from above at **p. s. r.**; and this water strikes at **s.** and at **r.** and then falls into **u. v.** and from **u.** returns to **s.**, and thus it strikes continuously upon this wheel and increases the force because of the weights that rest upon it.

-> this method cannot be said to be "moved by water" <-
-> but by good reason should be called "moved by weights of water" <-

And that this is true, the eye proves by the example I have placed above. In the example **t. s.** and through **r. f.**, the water goes through the inside of the wheel and fills it while at **i.** it is above, while at the <unclear>bottom</unclear> <note>original: "l'u."</note> of it are **s. p. f.** and of **r. i. u.**

---

-> as it appears inside the millstone <term>mola</term> of a mill <-

A hollow circle into which the wheel of a forked-gear <note>original: "furcho." Likely a reference to a lantern gear or a specific forked mechanical component within the mill's transmission.</note> enters; and as the wheel travels through the inside, it creates a space so that there is no disturbance <note>original: "tormento," implying turbulence or resistance that hinders the mechanism.</note> of water caused by it. And within these small channels <term>canaletti</term>, no calculation of water will occur along the straight path of the wheel, because the water clears itself out and forms itself there; for this reason, it should be called a "fork-wheel" <note>original: "rrota furcho."</note>. While this wheel was <unclear>turning</unclear>, it would not become <unclear>clogged</unclear> with water, except for that which is there by reason of the casing of the vessel and the water within, just as one sees inside a small box <note>original: "chassetta."</note> of water.

<summary>Leonardo analyzes the mechanical physics of water wheels, distinguishing between the force of moving water (impact) and the force of the water's weight. He also provides technical notes on the internal housing of millstones to reduce water turbulence and mechanical wear.</summary>

<keywords>hydraulics, water wheel, mill machinery, mechanical force, friction, gravity, gear design, fluid dynamics</keywords>

--- Page 155 ---
<meta>Continuity with page 53: On the previous page, Leonardo explored the mechanics of water wheels and how the weight and impact of water generate force. On this page, he shifts from the theory of mechanical motion to the practical industry of metallurgy. He provides specific instructions for the cleaning, finishing, and casting of bronze, likely for the production of large artillery pieces like bombards.</meta>

### Plate 47, folio 17 r. — (52)

->How one should clean castings<-
Make a bundle <term>mazo</term> <note>A brush or scrubber made of bundled materials.</note> of thick iron wire, like thin twine, and you shall scrub with water, keeping a tub underneath so that you do not make mud on the floor.

->How to remove the burrs from the bronze<-
Make an iron rod used like a wide chisel <term>scarpello</term>, and with it you shall rub over those ridges <note>original: "creste," literally "crests."</note> of the bronze that remain on the castings <term>gietti</term> of the bombards <note>A bombard is a large, front-loading cannon used during the 15th and 16th centuries.</note> which result from the cracks <note>original: "ischiappature," referring to the seams where molten metal leaked into gaps in the mold.</note> in the mold. Make sure that the rod is heavy and that the strokes are long and forceful.

->Ease of melting<-
Alloy a portion of the metal in the forehearth <note>original: "manicha," likely referring to the furnace flue or a pre-heating chamber.</note>, then put it into the furnace; and this will initiate, with its molten "bath," the melting of the copper.

->To provide for the copper if it should cool in the furnace<-
If the copper should cool in the furnace, as soon as you notice it, make sure to cut it with pokers <note>original: "frugatoi," long iron rods used to stir the molten metal.</note> while it is still in a mash <note>original: "paniccia," a culinary term for a thick porridge, used here to describe the semi-solid state of the metal as it cools.</note>; or, if it has cooled entirely, cut it as one does lead with heavy and thick chisels.

->If you had to make a large casting<-
If you had to make a casting of one hundred thousand pounds, do it with 5 furnaces of 2,000 pounds each, or up to 3,000 pounds at most. <note>Note: Leonardo's math here seems inconsistent with the total weight mentioned, or perhaps he is referring to the rate of feed or specific crucible batches within a larger operation.</note>

<summary>This page outlines practical metallurgical techniques for cleaning bronze artillery, managing the melting process in a furnace, and handling large-scale castings.</summary>
<keywords>bronze casting, bombards, metallurgy, furnace maintenance, copper, tools, industrial techniques</keywords>

--- Page 156 ---
<meta>Continuity with page 53: On the previous page, Leonardo discussed the cleaning and refinement of bronze castings and how to manage copper in the furnace. This page continues the practical instructions for the foundry, focusing on the construction of the casting templates, methods for breaking down large pieces of metal, and a sequential technique for casting multiple light cannons simultaneously.</meta>

# Plate 48, folio 16 v. — (53)

### How the template board for the bombard should be
The board <term>asse</term> <note>A "strickle" or template board used to shape the clay of the mold as it rotates.</note> that forms the bombard should be made of pearwood <term>pero</term> <note>Pearwood was preferred by Renaissance engineers for its stability and fine grain, which prevented warping.</note> and doubled in thickness from the middle to the back so that it does not twist. Where you leave the imprint of the moldings and the shape of the bombard on this board, it should be a flat face and not a sharp edge. When you apply the tallow <term>seuo</term> <note>Animal fat used as a lubricant and release agent.</note> with it, burnish that face with a pig's tooth <note>A traditional tool used by craftsmen to polish surfaces to a high shine.</note> so that it is smooth; the tallow should be finely strained so that it does not leave marks during the turning <note>The process of rotating the mold against the template board to create a perfectly cylindrical shape.</note>.

### How to break a large mass of bronze
If you wish to break a large mass of bronze, first suspend it. Then build a brick wall around it on four sides in the manner of a trough <term>truogo</term>. Build a great fire within, and when the bronze is glowing red, deliver blows to it with a heavy weight raised high and dropped with great force.

### On casting many "passavolanti" at once
<note>A *passavolante* (literally "fly-by") was a long, thin-barreled piece of light artillery designed for long-range shots.</note>
Prepare the channels for the bronze as shown here, and keep the points *d*, *b*, and *c* plugged. Leave the entire path for *a* open. When that one is full, unplug *b*; when that is full, unplug *c*, and then *d*. The mouth of the channels should be three fingers thick of brick and well-coated with ash <term>incianerato</term> <note>original: "incianerato." This refers to a protective wash of ash and water applied to the channels to prevent the molten metal from sticking to the brick.</note>. Then, the branches of the channels are removed with tongs <term>tanaglie</term> while they are still molten; they should be divided with iron plates covered in earth before the metal sets.

->How the cores of the bombards should be placed on the trestle<-
<note>original: "a chavalo," literally "on a horse." This refers to the horizontal frame or "lathe" used to support the heavy core of the cannon mold.</note>

->How the wood should be positioned before the thick rope is applied<-
<note>In the casting process, a wooden spindle was wrapped in straw or thick hemp rope to provide a textured surface for the clay mold to adhere to.</note>

<summary>This page provides technical specifications for artillery production, including the use of pearwood templates for precision, a method for breaking scrap bronze using thermal shock, and a sequential manifold system for casting multiple cannons in a single pour.</summary>
<keywords>bombard, bronze casting, pearwood, tallow, passavolanti, foundry techniques, metallurgy, mold making</keywords>
