--- Page 166 ---
<meta>This page shifts from the linguistic and philosophical lists of the previous folio to technical and mathematical calculations regarding ballistics. Leonardo is determining the specific gravity and weight of projectiles made from various materials, moving from abstract vocabulary to the practical physics of warfare and engineering.</meta>

->(Of the making of iron balls)<-

The weight of an iron ball with a diameter of 1 inch <note>original: "oncia." In the Florentine system, 1 oncia is 1/12 of a braccio, roughly 4.8 cm.</note> is 6 2/3 ounces. 
The weight of one cubic cubit <note>original: "braccio." The Florentine cubit used here is approximately 58.3 cm.</note> of iron weighs 4440 pounds.

->(To know the diameter of any ball)<-

The Alexandria cubit is 1 cubit and 4 1/2 inches of Florence.
1 inch is 1/12 of a cubit.

<margin>
<note>Technical diagrams showing a cross-section of a mechanical assembly and a plan-view of a base or carriage structure with four corner supports.</note>
</margin>

->(Of the making of stone balls)<-

Stone is 2 2/3 times lighter than iron.
And therefore, a stone ball is 2 2/3 times larger than an iron ball of equal weight.

A stone ball with a diameter of 1 inch weighs 2 1/2 ounces.
A ball of 1 cubit of stone weighs 1665 pounds.
If the stone ball has a diameter of 1 cubit, it weighs 1665 pounds.
If the iron ball has a diameter of 1 cubit, it weighs 4440 pounds.
An iron ball with a diameter of 1/2 cubit weighs 555 pounds <note>Leonardo is applying the cube law: halving the diameter reduces the volume and weight to 1/8th of the original (4440 ÷ 8 = 555).</note>.
A stone ball with a diameter of 1/2 cubit weighs 208 1/8 pounds.

| 4440 | 555 | 1/8 |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| 1665 | 208 1/8 | 1/8 |
| 1000 | 125 | 1/8 |

->(To make lead balls)<-

Lead weighs 1 1/2 times more than iron, plus 1/16 more.
Lead weighs 4 times more than stone, plus 1/16 more.
A lead ball with a diameter of 1 inch weighs 10 1/8 ounces.
A lead ball with a diameter of 1 cubit weighs 6937 1/2 pounds.

->(Of the making of brass balls)<-

Brass <note>original: "ottone." In this context, it may refer generally to copper alloys used in artillery casting.</note> weighs one-sixth more than iron.
A brass ball of 1 inch weighs 7 7/12 ounces.
A brass ball of 1 cubit weighs 5180 pounds.

<summary>Leonardo records detailed mathematical ratios and weights for cannonballs made of iron, stone, lead, and brass. He demonstrates an understanding of the relationship between a projectile's diameter and its volume/weight, specifically noting that halving the diameter reduces the weight to one-eighth.</summary>
<keywords>ballistics, artillery, weights and measures, iron, stone, lead, brass, geometry, cubic cubit, Florentine measures</keywords>

--- Page 167 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo's technical studies from the previous folio, shifting from the specific gravity of materials (stone, iron, lead) to the practical application of these materials in metallurgy and the casting of artillery. Leonardo details how to economize bronze production and provides precise mathematical ratios for the construction of bombards and light cannons.</meta>

### On joining lead with the other metal <note>In the context of Renaissance engineering and casting, "metallo" usually refers specifically to bronze, an alloy of copper and tin.</note>

If you wish, for the sake of economy <note>original: "masseritia," meaning thrift or cost-saving measures.</note>, to put lead into the bronze to compensate for the amount of tin that is required in the bronze, first alloy the lead with the tin, and then place it upon the molten copper.

### How the furnace should be founded

The furnace must be between 4 well-founded pillars.

### On the thickness of the mantle <note>original: "chappa." This refers to the outer shell or "cope" of the mold used in casting large objects like cannons or bells.</note>

The mantle should not exceed the thickness of 2 fingers, and it must be layered four times over the fine earth, and then well reinforced; or rather, only baked from the inside and then thinly coated with cow ash <note>original: "cenere bouina." Ash or calcined material was used as a "parting agent" to prevent the molten metal from sticking to the mold.</note>.

### On the thickness of the bombard

The bombard should be for a ball of 600 pounds and upwards. With this rule, you shall make the measurement of the diameter of the ball, and divide that into 6 parts; and 1 of these parts shall be the thickness at the front, and at the back always half as much more. And if the ball is 700 pounds, 1/7th of the diameter of the ball shall be its thickness at the front; and if the ball is 800, 1/8th of its diameter at the front; and if 900, 1/8 and 1/2; and if 1000, 1/9th.

### On the length of the barrel <note>original: "tronba," literally "trumpet" or "tube," referring to the bore of the cannon.</note> of the bombard

If you want it to throw a stone ball, make the length of the barrel 6 or up to 7 balls [long]. And if the ball is made of iron, make the said barrel up to 12 balls long. And if the ball is made of lead, you shall make it up to eighteen balls. I say this for when the bombard has a mouth capable of receiving a stone ball of 600 pounds or more.

### On the thickness of the *passavolanti* <note>The *passavolante* was a type of long, light-caliber cannon designed for range and speed of fire.</note>

The thickness at the front of the *passavolanti* must not exceed from a half to a third of the diameter of the ball. And the length [should be] from 30 up to 36 balls.

<summary>This page outlines technical specifications for metallurgy and artillery engineering, including instructions for alloying lead into bronze and calculating the structural proportions of bombards and light cannons based on projectile weight.</summary>
<keywords>metallurgy, bronze casting, lead, tin, copper, furnace construction, mantle mold, bombard, passavolanti, ballistics, mathematical ratios</keywords>

### Table 50 / Folio 15 v. — (55)

-> The furnace <-
-> On coating the inside of the furnace <-
Before you put the metal into the furnace, it must be coated <note>original: "illotato," referring to the process of "luting" or sealing a surface with a clay-based paste.</note> with <term>Valencia earth</term> <note>A specialized refractory clay prized by Renaissance engineers for its ability to withstand extreme temperatures without cracking.</note>, and over that, ash.

---

-> On restoring the metal when it tries to cool <-
When you see the bronze starting to congeal <note>original: "chongielare." In metallurgy, this refers to the metal losing its fluidity and beginning to solidify before it can be poured.</note>, take willow wood split into thin pieces, and make a fire with them.

---

-> The cause of the congealing <-
I say that the cause of this congealing often stems from too much fire <note>Excessive heat can cause certain components of the alloy to oxidize or "burn off," changing the melting point.</note>, and also from wood that is not well-dried.

---

-> How to recognize the condition of the fire <-
You will know when the fire is good and useful by its clear flames; but if you see the tips of those flames are murky and end with much smoke, do not trust it, especially when you have the <term>bath</term> <note>The pool of molten metal inside the furnace.</note> almost <term>liquefied</term> <note>original: "inacq.ª" (in acqua), literally "in water." A technical term for metal reaching a fully liquid state.</note>.

---

-> Which woods are good <-
The woods are good when they are of young willow; or if you cannot have willow, you shall take alder, and let each be young and well-dried.

---

-> On alloying the metal <-
The metal for <term>bombards</term> <note>Large, early forms of cannons used primarily for sieges.</note> should generally be made with 6 or up to 8 percent—that is, 6 parts of tin for every hundred of copper—and the less of it you put in, the safer the bombard will be. <insert>*Which tin you shall put*</insert>

---

-> When the tin should be joined with the copper <-
The tin must be added to the copper when you have brought the copper to a liquid state.

---

-> How the melting should be accelerated <-
The melting will be accelerated by you when two-thirds of the copper has been liquefied; then, with a thick branch of chestnut wood, you shall stir the remainder of the copper that is still whole into the melted portion.

It specifies the ideal bronze alloy for cannons (6-8% tin) and techniques for stirring the melt to ensure uniform liquefaction.</summary>
<keywords>metallurgy, bronze casting, Valencia earth, willow wood, tin-copper alloy, bombards, furnace management, Renaissance engineering</keywords>

--- Page 169 ---
<meta>While the previous page focused on the practical metallurgy of casting bronze and managing a furnace, this folio marks a transition into Leonardo’s theoretical studies of fluid dynamics. He moves from the behavior of molten metal to the physics of air and wind, applying similar principles of weight, density, and momentum to the atmosphere.</meta>

### (On air and winds)

The motion of air is more effective <note>original: "migiore," literally 'better,' but here meaning more efficient or forceful.</note> lengthwise than transversely, because the <term>pulses</term> <note>original: "polsi." Leonardo often compared the movement of air and blood to the rhythmic beating of a pulse.</note> of the air are continuous, whereas transverse winds are interrupted.

### (On air joined with water)

The motion of air strikes the water with greater effect if the air is denser, since it is then heavier.

### (On air and its weight)

The weight of air is what moves it, and it moves with that weight which it receives from air made denser by its own weight. <note>Leonardo is exploring the concept of atmospheric pressure and how air density affects its movement.</note>

### (On air and its motion)

The principle of the motion of air is the same as that of water; water <term>plunges deeper</term> <note>original: "s'approfo<unclear>n</unclear>da."</note> the more it strikes the water over which it flows without the intervention of air; and the heavier the air is, the deeper the air plunges.

### (Of the wind among the waves)

The wind that is found among the waves of the water will have a more effective motion and yield a greater result <note>original: "rẽdimëto" (rendimento), referring to the output or efficiency of the force.</note> the more it is supported by the density of the air.

### (On the weight of air)

The heavier the air, the easier its transverse motion will be, because the motion of the air toward the earth will be more like <term>interrupted winds</term> <note>original: "vëti i<unclear>n</unclear>terrotti," likely referring to turbulent or gusty winds as opposed to a steady flow.</note>, just as a heavier object descends more forcefully.

### (On air and its weight)

The motion of air that descends is more effective than that which moves transversely (as it is of heavier matter).

---

<margin>
3
—
m
</margin>

The denser the air, the heavier the air, and thus its transverse motion will be easier; because the motion of the air toward the earth will be more like interrupted winds, because the motion of the air toward the earth will be more like interrupted winds. <note>Leonardo frequently repeated sentences in his notebooks, likely as he refined his thoughts or practiced his mirror-writing.</note>

<vocab>air, winds, motion, weight, density, water, interrupted, transverse, plunges deeper, yield</vocab>

<summary>This page contains Leonardo da Vinci’s theoretical observations on the physics of air, specifically how density and weight dictate the force and direction of wind. He compares the movement of air to that of water, suggesting that heavier, denser air moves more forcefully and interacts more powerfully with the surface of the sea.</summary>

<keywords>fluid dynamics, atmospheric pressure, wind patterns, air density, Leonardo da Vinci, physics of water, aerodynamics</keywords>

--- Page 172 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s exploration of the physics of air, transitioning from atmospheric winds to the "internal winds" of the human body—specifically how air produces sound and the mechanics of involuntary respiratory actions. This reflects his method of applying macroscopic physical laws to microscopic human anatomy.</meta>

### ->(On the voice from above and from below)<-

If you were in the middle of the air and shouted to a companion who was in a valley, and if you were in a valley and shouted to a companion who was on a mountain, it would seem to the one in the valley that the voice of the one on the mountain was much <term>thicker</term> <note>original: "grossa." In this context, Leonardo refers to a sound that is deeper, louder, or more resonant.</note> than it actually is. To the one on the mountain, it would seem that the voice of the one in the valley was much <term>thinner and sharper</term> <note>original: "sottile e acuta," meaning higher in pitch or more piercing.</note>. This occurs because the voice of the one on the mountain descends with <term>weight</term> <note>Leonardo theorized that sound, being a movement of air, was influenced by gravity. He believed sound traveling downward gained force from the "weight" of the air, while sound traveling upward was weakened by it.</note>, and the voice from the valley travels against that weight, as will be proven in the margin. For this reason, hunters are accustomed to standing above, so that their voices may be well heard by the dogs and likewise by the other hunters.

---

### ->(On laughter)<-

Laughter arises from a certain dilation of the mouth as it is pulled inward, and consequently, there follows a tightening of the eyes.

---

### ->(On crying)<-

Crying arises from the opening of the mouth, the tightening of the forehead, the nose, and the eyes, and the lifting of the lips.

---

### ->(On shouting)<-

Shouting is caused by the pushing of air from within the lungs through the <term>throat</term> <note>original: "stroza," an archaic Italian term for the windpipe or larynx.</note>, which air is driven out in a sudden burst by the force of the lungs.

---

### ->(On one who laughs)<-

One who laughs does not feel their breath being "stolen" <note>meaning they do not feel short of breath or gasping.</note>, but one who cries certainly does, because laughter is briefer and more broken.

---

### ->(On yawning)<-

Yawning arises from the fatigue of the mouth and the lungs, because the air that is inside needs to be renewed. Since the lungs cannot <term>recharge</term> <note>original: "richaricharsi."</note> themselves with air as quickly as they do when a person is not tired, the person then opens the whole mouth to drive out the <term>stale air</term> <note>original: "aria viziata," literally 'vitiated' or 'spoiled' air. Leonardo intuitively understood that the body needed to expel "used" air and take in fresh oxygen.</note> and take in the new.

---

### ->(On coughing)<-

Coughing arises through the force of air which, wanting to exit the lungs, finds the throat closed and pushes so much that it eventually forces it open.

<summary>Leonardo analyzes how gravity affects the pitch and volume of the human voice and provides mechanical descriptions of physiological expressions such as laughing, crying, and yawning.</summary>
<keywords>acoustics, vocal mechanics, laughter, crying, yawning, anatomy of the throat, respiratory system, atmospheric weight</keywords>

<meta>This page marks a transition from Leonardo’s observations on acoustics and human anatomy to his technical studies on military engineering. He shifts here to the practical chemistry and metallurgy required for casting "bombards" (large-caliber cannons), specifically focusing on the composition of the casting molds and the preparation of the core.</meta>

folio 16 r. — (56)
169
Plate 51.

### ->The fine earth for the bombards<-

Take the shearing-earth <note>original: "tera di cimatura." This refers to clay mixed with the fine clippings or "shearings" left over from the cloth-making process. The wool fibers acted as a binding agent to prevent the clay mold from cracking during the heat of casting.</note> and smear it onto the wall in thin layers so that it may dry thoroughly. Afterward, crush it and sift it into a fine powder. To 50 parts of this powder, add 10 parts of crushed brick <note>original: "quadriello."</note>, which should not be over-fired, and let it also be well crushed and sifted. Add also a little fine shearings or lint from fustian cloths. Furthermore, to this composition, add 6 parts of ash, which you shall sift and liquefy with heavily salted water. Apply this with a painter’s brush in 2 or 3 thin, liquid coats, letting it dry each time without the use of fire. It would also be beneficial to first apply a layer of burned ox dung liquefied with salt water.

---

### ->On the tallow<-

The tallow <note>original: "sevo." Animal fat used as a lubricant or a "release agent" to prevent the different layers of the mold from sticking together.</note> should be applied together with smithy soot <note>original: "filigine di fabro."</note> as fine as you can make it, or with cow ash.

---

### ->On the moldings<-

The moldings <note>original: "chornici." These are the decorative rings or architectural details on the exterior of the cannon.</note> should be formed almost to the end using a small cord, such as those used for spinning tops. Finish the moldings over that cord with fine earth, and polish them with the aforementioned tallow and soot. The decorative ornaments should be made of wax.

---

### ->The core<-

The tail of the core <term>mascio</term> <note>The "male" part of the mold; a cylindrical internal structure around which the bronze is poured to create the hollow bore of the cannon.</note> must finally be covered with powdered brick and ash mixed with salt water. Ash made from cow dung, applied with salt water over the said core, is even better—indeed, it is superior.

---

### ->On managing the core<-

The core must first be placed into the casting pit with hooks, as you have seen before. Then, fire it inch by inch <note>original: "oncia a oncia." Literally "ounce by ounce," but here referring to small, progressive increments of heat.</note>, working your way upward until it reaches the color of a fired brick. Then, test it with a small stick, striking it gently part by part; where you hear it ring <note>Leonardo is checking for structural integrity or air pockets by listening to the resonance of the fired clay.</note>, bind it with iron wire. To ensure no mistakes are made, you should rotate it completely.

---

### ->On the universal earth<-

The universal earth <note>The general-purpose clay used for the bulk of the mold, rather than the specialized finishing layers.</note> should be the same kind used to make bricks, mixed with cow dung and wool cloth shearings.

<summary>This page provides technical recipes and procedural instructions for preparing the clay molds and internal cores used in casting large cannons. Leonardo details the specific mixtures of clay, cloth fibers, ash, and salt water needed to create a heat-resistant and precise casting environment.</summary>
<keywords>cannon casting, metallurgy, mold making, bombards, engineering, materials science, Leonardo da Vinci, masonry, acoustics in engineering</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the practical instructions on casting bombards found on the previous page to a literary and philosophical exercise. Leonardo here records an anecdote about the philosopher Demetrius and a list of sophisticated vocabulary, likely used for his own self-study to improve his writing style.</meta>

fol. 14 v. — (57)
# Plate 52.

Demetrius <note>Demetrius the Cynic (c. 1st century AD), known for his sharp tongue and criticism of the unlearned and powerful.</note> used to say that there was no difference between the words and the voice of the unskilled and ignorant and the sounds and noises caused by a belly filled with superfluous wind. And he said this not without reason, for he did not consider there to be any difference from which part they emitted their voice: whether from the lower parts or from the mouth, since the one and the other were of equal value and substance.

---

Folly is a shield for shame, just as impudence is for poverty.

### Plate 54.
#### fol. 30 r. — (59)

The bottom of the furnace [consists of] 3 layers of raw bricks of common earth and 1 1/2 finger-widths of ash.
The vault [consists of] only 1 [layer] of raw Valencia bricks <note>original: "ualenza"; high-quality, heat-resistant bricks from Valencia were standard for furnace construction</note> and 1 other single layer of fired bricks.

The calyxes <note>likely the crucibles or small molds</note> are placed with the ash.

The wood for the core of the bombards <term>bombard: a large-caliber medieval cannon</term> should be covered with ash one finger thick.

The mouth of the furnace—that is, where the flame enters—must be made of large Valencia bricks.

Each of the 2 chimneys should be half the size of the window where the flame enters.

<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s linguistic self-education in the Codex Trivulzianus. Having previously listed technical terms and abstract nouns, he here expands into extensive lists of synonyms and related concepts ranging from musical harmony and moral virtues to various forms of violence and social status. This folio is famous for the juxtaposition of these academic word-lists with a vivid grotesque caricature, showing how Leonardo’s study of language and his study of the human face were both methods of categorizing the world.</meta>

### Folio 40 recto

Seeing the branches of an oak trunk or of a tree if it is thick with leaves:
the branches are denser toward the center relative to one another than in the middle of a very thick forest of trees.
The shaded part is darker in the middle of a forest than in a single tree.
That tree among trees is darkest in its center where the branches cannot be seen.
The foliage of a tree where one sees the center will be brighter in the center than [foliage] seen in the center of denser ones.

--- Page 184 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s linguistic exercises from the Codex Trivulzianus. As seen on the previous page, Leonardo was not only interested in the mechanics of the natural world but also in the mechanics of language. He often used these lists to broaden his vocabulary, practicing the "volgare" (the common Italian vernacular) and incorporating Latinisms to improve his ability to write with the elegance and precision of the scholars of his time. This page shifts from thematic nouns to a massive catalogue of verbs, organized roughly by their starting letter.</meta>

<note>Leonardo compiled these lists as a personal dictionary. In the Renaissance, the ability to express a single idea through many different synonyms was a mark of a sophisticated writer. Here, he focuses on verbs of action, thought, and emotion, starting with the letters A, D, I, P, and F.</note>

<term>to connect <note>original: "annectere"</note></term> — A Latinate verb meaning to join or link things together. Leonardo often used Latin-derived terms to elevate his scientific and literary descriptions.

<term>to reach <note>original: "atigniere"</note></term> — Specifically used in the context of drawing water from a well or reaching for a high goal.

<term>to destroy <note>original: "disdruere"</note></term> — An archaic form of *distruggere*. Leonardo's spelling often reflects the regional dialects and older Italian forms of the 15th century.

<term>to fill <note>original: "implire"</note></term> — From the Latin *implere*.

<term>to steal <note>original: "inuolare"</note></term> — Literally to "fly away with" something; to snatch or steal.

<term>to discern <note>original: "discernere"</note></term> — To perceive or recognize the difference between things, a crucial skill for an artist and scientist.

<term>to absolve <note>original: "asoluere"</note></term> — To set free from an obligation or sin.

<term>to annihilate <note>original: "anichilare"</note></term> — To reduce to nothing (from *nihil*, nothing).

<summary>This folio consists of an extensive, alphabetized list of Italian and Latinate verbs. It serves as evidence of Leonardo da Vinci’s self-directed education as he sought to master the literary language of the Italian Renaissance.</summary>

<keywords>vocabulary, linguistics, Codex Trivulzianus, Renaissance Italian, verbs, synonyms, self-education, Latinisms</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s linguistic exercises from the Codex Trivulzianus. As seen on the previous page, Leonardo was building a personal lexicon, collecting synonyms and complex terms to elevate his writing from the common "volgare" to a more scholarly, Latinate style. This specific page focuses on a wide array of abstract nouns, adjectives, and verbs related to morality, law, and physical sensations.</meta>

### Table 56.
#### fol. 31 r. — (61)

<margin>concerning the drop of a river</margin>
If you wish to know the drop <note>the vertical descent or gradient</note> of a river for every mile without using any other leveling instrument, you shall follow this method: look for a part of the river that is most representative of the general quantity and flow of the river whose drop you wish to know. Of that part, you shall take 100 cubits <note>original: "br." (braccia), a Renaissance unit of length approximately 23 inches</note> of the bank, marking their beginning and end with two markers as shown above at points **a** and **b**. Then, at the beginning, place a bladder, a gall-nut, or a small piece of cork, and note in how many musical beats <note>Leonardo used the rhythm of music as a makeshift stopwatch to measure time</note> that object travels along the downward waves to the end of the 100-cubit path. Afterward, measure many other objects in slower and even faster currents, and then measure the drop of the 100 cubits with a level. With this calculation, having measured various waters, you will then be able to tell the drop per mile simply by taking 100 paces along a bank and noting in how many beats your floating object completed that course.

--- Page 210 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the lexical lists seen on the previous page to Leonardo’s scientific investigations into acoustics. Having practiced his vocabulary, he now applies his precision of language to describe the physical nature of sound, percussion, and vibration.</meta>

fol. 36 r. — (69)

# Table 64

-> centered text <-
What sound is and how it is created by a blow

*The blow and the*
The time in which a blow is generated is the shortest thing that can be done by man. And there is no body so large that, if it is suspended, it will not immediately move when struck by a brief blow; this movement strikes back against the air, and the air—touching the moved object—sounds.

-> centered text <-
Whether the noise is in the hammer or in the anvil

<margin>On the sound of the hammer with the anvil</margin>
I say that because the anvil is not suspended, it cannot resonate. The hammer resonates <note>original: "resona"; Leonardo uses this to mean the ringing or sustain of a sound.</note> in the bounce it makes after the blow. If the anvil were to ring, the sound made in it by every small hammer would have the same pitch <note>original: "basseza di boce," literally "lowness of voice," referring here to musical pitch.</note>, just as a bell maintains the same pitch regardless of what strikes it. Thus, the anvil would do the same when struck by different hammers. But since you hear different "voices" (pitches) depending on the various sizes of the hammers, it follows that the voice is in the hammer and not in the anvil.

-> centered text <-
Why an object that is not suspended does not sound
-> centered text <-
And if it is suspended, every small contact
-> centered text <-
takes the sound away from it

A struck bell creates an immediate <term>vibration<gloss>original: "triemito," meaning a rapid trembling or oscillation</gloss></term>, and this sudden vibration immediately strikes the surrounding air, which immediately resonates. If the vibration is hindered by even the smallest contact, the bell does not tremble, it does not strike the air, and the air does not resonate.

-> centered text <-
Whether the bird that suddenly strikes the air
-> centered text <-
should make it resonate or not

I say no, because the air that penetrates through the thing that strikes it <note>Leonardo is likely referring to the porous nature of feathers.</note> does not receive a "blow"; therefore, it cannot make a sound.

-> centered text <-
Of the <term>bombard<gloss>An early type of large-caliber cannon used in the 14th to 17th centuries.</gloss></term> or the arrow

Here, the motion of the air sounds because it is more powerful than the resisting air.

<vocab>blow, sound, hammer, anvil, air, bell, vibration, bombard, arrow</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo analyzes the physics of acoustics, concluding that sound is the result of rapid vibrations striking the air. He uses the specific example of a hammer and anvil to argue that the source of a sound's pitch resides in the vibrating object (the hammer) rather than the stationary one (the anvil).</summary>
<keywords>acoustics, vibration, physics of sound, percussion, resonance, anvil and hammer, aerodynamics</keywords>

--- Page 211 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s exploration of physical forces, shifting from the acoustics and percussion discussed on the previous page to the related field of atmospheric physics and friction. Leonardo often linked these topics, seeing the vibration of a hammer blow and the resistance of air as part of the same study of "shocks" or "impacts" between bodies.</meta>

-> Reasoning on the impact of bodies against bodies <-

The motion of bodies whose surfaces are heated by the fast friction of the air; they wear away <note>original: "si chōsumano," meaning to consume or erode through wear</note> so much more, one than the other, in proportion to how much the air in that place is denser, or rather faster. Or truly, the thinner the air and the slower the air, the more the motion heats up <note>Leonardo is observing the relationship between air density, speed, and aerodynamic heating, a concept that would later be central to high-speed physics.</note>.

<margin>
On the motion of the hammer with the anvil
</margin>

Regarding the manner of the wind's movement: it will not be equal in every part, but will be greater <note>original: "mã nore," a contraction for "maggiore"</note> above than below, because the lower part, being impeded by the ruggedness <note>original: "rāgoli," likely referring to the uneven obstacles or angles of the terrain</note> of the earth, will be slower the closer it is to the ground. The upper air will be faster the more remote it is from said earth. This upper air, with its velocity, compresses the lower air, and the lower air then turns back on itself and creates <term>turbulence<gloss>original: "ruina," literally a ruin or collapse, used by Leonardo to describe the chaotic breakdown of air or water flow</gloss></term>, being worn away by that velocity.

The smallest parts of air contained between the stones of a stream of water become so much thicker as the air there is slower, due to that air which turns the lower air with its velocity.

All things near the stones, gradually to the parts of air...
...of the air through that velocity of the air remote from those stones...
...that velocity of remote air...
...insofar as the sense of the air...

The parts of air gradually become slower, little by little, as they...

---

<summary>Leonardo examines the physics of air resistance and wind behavior, noting that friction generates heat on moving surfaces and that wind speed increases with altitude as ground obstacles create turbulence and drag.</summary>
<keywords>atmospheric physics, air resistance, friction, heat, wind speed gradient, turbulence, aerodynamics, fluid dynamics</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the previous folio's philosophical and ethical categorizations (Table 65) back to Leonardo’s core scientific investigations into mechanics, hydraulics, and the mathematics of art. While the previous page dealt with "movements of the soul," this folio, Table 66, focuses on the physical "nature of impact" and the paradoxes of optical perspective.</meta>

fol. 37 r. — (71)
Table 66.

## Table 66.

->on impact<-

Every object that is struck against a resisting object rebounds from that object at an angle equal to that of the impact. <note>This is a fundamental law of physics: the angle of incidence equals the angle of reflection.</note>

---

->note on waters<-

This same principle is proven in the tenth proposition of the third book of *The Nature of Impact* <note>original: "del cholpo," referring to Leonardo's planned but never fully published treatise on mechanics</note>, where it treats the ball struck against a wall. And if you wished to know the depth of a waterfall, look at the line of the fall at point *c-b* and determine the quality of obliquity <note>the angle or slant</note> it possesses. Then, look at the width between the point of impact *b* and the resurgence *a* <note>original: "surgimento," the point where the water bubbles back up after hitting the bottom</note>, and form the angle *a-b-d* and measure it, just as is demonstrated in the tenth [proposition] on impacts. 

And if you were of the opinion that the water in this case might deceive you because of some opposing object interposed in the line of the rebound, know that if the waterfall is old <note>long-established</note>, it will have worn away every opposition that stood against its surging — — —

->perspective<-

I ask you, painter: why do the figures made by you not appear in a diminished form through the demonstration of perspective? Why do they not appear, at an equal distance, as large as natural figures of the same size placed next to those painted upon the wall? — — —

And why do things appearing at a small distance, at an equal distance, appear larger than the natural [figures]?

->unresolved question for a painter<-

**impact, percussion, waters, proposition, waterfall, perspective, painter, distance, natural**

<summary>Leonardo applies the geometric law of reflection (angle of incidence equals angle of reflection) to both physical impacts and the movement of water, while posing challenging questions about the optical illusions inherent in painting and perspective.</summary>
<keywords>mechanics, reflection, hydraulics, waterfalls, optics, perspective, painting theory, geometry of water</keywords>

And if a weight were suspended at .a. from a cord .ab. which is
held over .b. and .c., and if in this cord there is a weight at .d.
Move that weight to .e. I ask: whether the cord .bd. would be shorter

from the length of .be., I say: that if .bd. is equal to .be.,
then .e. would be lower than .d. And if it were shorter, .e. would be

### Architecture

The column that will appear thicker will be that which is suspended
in the air <note>likely referring to a column on an upper story or one seen against the bright sky</note> above the column of the temple <note>the lower order of a building</note>. Above that column,
let it appear to be of the same height. So it shall be: the one that is
higher shall be flat <note>referring to the lack of entasis or a specific surface treatment</note>; the one that is lower...

If the column were equal in thickness from top to bottom, that
would be according to the rules of perspective <note>meaning that, to the viewer's eye, it would appear to taper or diminish as it rises</note>.

-> He will give me a receipt and he struck me <-
<note>original: "dirā mi rischicta ē m battē"; this appears to be a personal memorandum or a fragment of a legal/financial nature, possibly regarding a dispute, which Leonardo often jotted in the margins of his scientific notes.</note>

<term>cord (corda)</term>: In Leonardo's mechanics, this refers to a flexible line used to study tension and geometry.
<term>weight (peso)</term>: A mass used to test the effects of gravity and motion.
<term>length (lungeza)</term>: Linear measurement.
<term>height (alteza)</term>: Vertical distance.
<term>column (colona)</term>: A vertical architectural support, a frequent subject of Leonardo's optical studies.
<term>temple (tempio)</term>: A generic term for a large classical building or church.
<term>perspective (prospettiua)</term>: The science of representing three-dimensional objects on a two-dimensional surface, or the study of how objects appear to the eye based on distance.
<term>architecture (architettura)</term>: The art and science of building.

<meta>This page transitions from the previous page's metaphorical word lists into a study of mechanics and statics. Leonardo shifts his focus to the mathematical relationship between weight, distance, and balance, a hallmark of his engineering studies.</meta>

### On the Balance of Weights

Weights of equal heaviness <note>original: "gravezza"; here Leonardo refers to the physical weight or the force of gravity acting on an object.</note>, where one is more distant from the center of the balance <note>original: "bilācia"; a scale or lever system.</note> than the other, will vary in their effective weights in proportion to their distances. These weights will be the same [in their effect] at the center of their balance.

-> note on painting <-

announcement <note>original: "annvrio"; likely a variant or misspelling of the Italian "annunzio," continuing Leonardo's habit of recording vocabulary.</note>

If a weight is suspended by two equal threads that are equidistant from each other, and are parallel and equal to each other, the weights will be:
+ . f . m . m . q n . r . v . r n r . v <note>These letter strings represent Leonardo's shorthand for geometric points or variables in his proof, correlating to the diagrams provided.</note>
and if they are parallel to each other and are equal, the weights will be:
p . m . m . q u r n r h u m n
equidistant . s . m r n l a

<summary>Leonardo examines the principles of statics, noting how the distance from a pivot point changes a weight's effective force, alongside geometric notes on suspended weights.</summary>
<keywords>statics, mechanics, balance, weight distribution, levers, physics, geometry</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from Leonardo’s mathematical proofs on balances and weights to his observations on the physics of perception and the theory of painting. He moves from the mechanical behavior of objects to how the human eye perceives their motion and depth, particularly the difference between seeing with one eye versus two.</meta>

### Plate 69

The movement of an object <note>original: "chosa"; Leonardo frequently uses "cosa" to refer to any physical body or object under observation.</note> near a stationary object often makes that stationary object appear to move with the motion of the moving object, while the moving object itself seems stationary and still.

->painting <note>original: "pittura"</note>      note on painting<-

Objects in relief <term>relief (rilievo)</term><note>In Leonardo’s time, "rilievo" referred to the three-dimensional quality of an object that makes it appear to stand out from a background.</note> viewed from nearby with only one eye <note>original: "solocio," a contraction of "solo occhio."</note> appear similar to a perfect painting. If you look with [both] eyes, *a* and *b*, at point *c*, that point *c* will appear to you as *d* and *f* <note>Leonardo is describing binocular disparity—the way each eye sees a slightly different view of an object.</note>. But if you look with only one eye, it will appear as *h* in *m* and *o*. A painting will never possess these two variations within itself <note>Leonardo identifies a fundamental limit of art: a flat painting cannot replicate the shifting perspectives provided by two eyes looking at a three-dimensional object.</note>.

movement, object, painting, relief, single eye, variation

<summary>Leonardo observes the optical illusion of relative motion and explains why paintings cannot fully replicate three-dimensional depth due to the mechanics of binocular vision.</summary>
<keywords>optics, perception, relative motion, binocular vision, painting theory, relief, monocular vision, depth perception</keywords>

--- Page 228 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s exploration of natural philosophy and optics. Having previously discussed how the mechanics of the eye differ from a flat painting, he now examines the physical forces of "necessity" and "power" (potential) that act upon elements like water, as well as the atmospheric effects that alter how the eye perceives color over distances.</meta>

### Plate 70, Folio 39 recto — (75)

Many th...
Many times the same thing <note>original: "chosa"; Leonardo frequently uses this word to denote any physical object or phenomenon under observation.</note> is pulled by two forces <note>original: "violentie"; in Renaissance physics, this refers to external forces acting upon an object, often contrasted with its "natural" motion.</note>, namely: necessity and power. Water falls as rain and the earth absorbs it by the necessity of moisture <note>original: "neciessita domore"; here, Leonardo suggests the earth has a natural, "necessary" affinity for water.</note>. The sun draws it up <note>original: "lassuele"; likely a variation of "assorbe" (absorbs) or "svellere" (to pull away), referring to evaporation.</note>, not by necessity, but by power <note>original: "potentia"; referring to the active force of the sun's heat overcoming the water's natural weight.</note>.

The medium that exists between the eye and the object seen transforms that object into its own color, just as the blue air makes distant mountains appear blue. Red glass makes whatever the eye sees behind it appear red. The light that stars create around themselves is held <note>original: "chechupato," likely a contraction of "che è occupato," meaning "is occupied" or "contained."</note> by the darkness of the night that is found between the eye and the illumination of that star.

->note on painting<-

<summary>Leonardo describes the dual forces of "necessity" and "power" acting on water and explains how the atmosphere acts as a colored medium that shifts the appearance of distant objects and stars.</summary>
<keywords>physics, necessity, power, evaporation, optics, atmospheric perspective, color theory, astronomy, perception</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s investigation into the physical properties of the senses. Moving from the optics and atmospheric perspective discussed on the previous page, he now turns to acoustics—the mechanics of how sound travels and is perceived by the ears—and concludes with a foundational concept of his art theory.</meta>

### Plate 75, Folio 15

A sound <note>original: "boce"; an archaic form of "voce." While it literally means "voice," Leonardo uses it here to refer to sound or a vocal note in a general acoustic sense.</note>, if it were divided between one ear and the other, so that one might hear it and the other not. Why should one hear it and the other not? 

This cannot be achieved by the sound itself, unless one ear were already without its hearing or were blocked by something. 

<margin>$\phi$ <note>The Greek letter phi, frequently used by Leonardo as a reference mark to link related notes or diagrams across different pages.</note></margin>
On the other hand, the sound shall be divided in the motion of the sound's "wind" <note>original: "uento della boce"; Leonardo often conceptualized sound as a physical displacement of air, similar to a breeze or wave.</note>, by which wind the said sound is carried through the specified air passages <note>original: "spiracoli"; Leonardo uses this term to describe small openings, vents, or conduits through which air—and thus sound—travels.</note>. Through these "winds," the said sound is divided within these air passages and is transformed, and the sound changes by means of these air passages.

->**<term>silent painting</term>** <note>original: "pittura muta"; this is a central theme in Leonardo's "Paragone" (Comparison of the Arts), where he famously described painting as "silent poetry" and poetry as "blind painting," arguing for the superiority of the visual over the verbal.</note><-

<summary>Leonardo examines whether sound can be directed to a single ear and theorizes that sound propagates like a "wind" through physical air passages. The page concludes with his famous definition of painting as a "silent" medium.</summary>
<keywords>acoustics, hearing, air passages, pittura muta, Paragone, philosophy of art, physics of sound</keywords>

### Folio 78r

the fish that enter for me through the "eyes" of the net <note>Leonardo refers to the mesh openings of a fishing net as "occhi" (eyes).</note>

the motion of that wave never stops until
the said water falls back down and strikes,

<meta>Continuing from Folio 78r, which discussed fluid dynamics and observations of water movement using silkworm cocoons, Leonardo now shifts to engineering designs for naval warfare, a philosophical reflection on the relationship between soul and body, an anecdote on youth and age, and a technical recipe for glass coloring.</meta>

### Plate 71

Ships made of beams
Ships made of woven wicker and covered in leather for **corsairs** <note>original: "corsali"; referring to privateers or pirates who required light, fast vessels.</note>

To strike at sea walls or towers, take the galleys, and before joining them together, pull the oars inside so that the sides touch one another; and with the oars on the outer side, you will navigate in this manner. It will appear as a single vessel upon the water; you will build towers and strong **bulwarks** <note>original: "bastia"; a fortification or temporary wooden defense structure.</note> upon it, capable of carrying all necessary artillery for battle.

The soul can never be corrupted by the corruption of the body, but it acts within the body in the likeness of the wind that causes the sound of the organ; if one pipe is broken, a good effect cannot be produced through that **void** <note>Leonardo is using a musical metaphor: the soul is the "wind" (the animator), and the body is the "organ" (the instrument). If the instrument is damaged, the music (life/action) is spoiled, though the wind remains unchanged.</note>.

->A motto said by a young man to an old man<-

An old man publicly scorning a young man, boldly showing that he did not fear him; whereupon the young man replied to him that his advanced age served as a better shield for him than his tongue or his strength.

->To make red in glass for flesh tones<-

Take rubies of **new rock** <note>original: "rocha nova"; likely referring to a specific mineral source or a type of synthetic "ruby" glass.</note> or garnets, and mix them with **lattimo** <note>original: "lattimo"; an opaque, milky-white glass pioneered in Venice to mimic porcelain.</note>; also, **Armenian bole** <note>original: "bolo armenio"; a reddish-brown clay traditionally used as a pigment or as a base for gold leaf.</note> is partially good.

<summary>This page features a diverse collection of Leonardo's notes, ranging from the design of armored naval vessels for sieges to a philosophical analogy comparing the soul to wind in an organ, alongside a recipe for creating flesh-colored glass.</summary>
<keywords>naval engineering, galleys, siege engines, philosophy of the soul, organ metaphor, anecdotes, glassmaking, pigments, lattimo, Armenian bole</keywords>

### Plate 72

Line **parallel** <note>original: "equidiacente"; Leonardo uses this term to describe a line that remains horizontal or parallel to a reference, indicating a state of equilibrium.</note> to a. b.

Place two weights, one at a. b. and the other at b. c.,
which weigh **proportionally** <note>original: "proportionevole"; referring to the mathematical ratio required between the mass of the weights and their distance from the fulcrum.</note>
to the aforementioned **partitions** <note>original: "partitioni"; the specific divisions or segments marked along the arm of the balance.</note>.
Then place the weight
from a into c, and that of c
into a, and the **balance** <note>original: "bilancie"; the weighing instrument or the beam itself.</note> will remain
along a parallel line. ——

<meta>This page transitions from the physical mechanics of equilibrium (Plate 72) into the abstract geometry of optics and vision. While the previous page dealt with tangible weights and balances, Folio 12r utilizes a shorthand symbolic system to map how points in space are perceived by the eye, a foundational study for his theories on perspective.</meta>

### Folio 12r (Plate 79)

. p . v . n . v . from the . point . v . p
<note>The abbreviations p. and v. represent *punto* (point) and *veduto* (seen or visible). This sequence likely charts the path of a sight line from the observer's point to a visible or hidden object.</note>

---
<note>Schematic diagram consisting of a horizontal line and a cross with letters.</note>
-> point / central <-
---

because every p. p. of n. v. v. . p. c. <note>original shorthand: "d d di n v v . p c"; likely "every part/point of the non-visible... central point."</note>
p . v . p . n . n . v . n . p . c
p . v . n . p . n . n . n . p . p . a . r . l . n
m . p . r . p . n . n . p . v . p . s . a . p . n
the eye . v . p . n . v . n . a . n . v . p . p . p . p . r . p . p
m . v . p . p . n . p . m . p . p . v . p
from point from the point v . r . r

<note>Leonardo’s shorthand in these lines is a dense logic puzzle regarding the geometry of vision. The letters represent technical terms: **p.** = point (*punto*), **v.** = visible (*veduto*), **n.** = not (*non*), **d.** = of/from (*di/da*), and **c.** = central (*centrale*). By arranging these, he calculates which points of an object are hidden and which are seen by **the eye** (*l'occhio*) based on their position relative to the central line of sight.</note>

<term>point</term> (punto), <term>the eye</term> (l'occhio), <term>visible</term> (veduto), <term>not visible</term> (non veduto), <term>perspective</term> (prospettiva)

<summary>This page features Leonardo’s cryptic shorthand for analyzing the laws of perspective. By using a symbolic code, he explores how points in space become visible or remain hidden from a specific viewpoint (the eye).</summary>
<keywords>optics, perspective, point of sight, visibility, geometry, shorthand, vision, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

--- Page 238 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the abstract, symbolic shorthand of optical geometry found on Folio 12r to a more structured investigation of the laws of physics. Here, Leonardo moves from how we see objects to the fundamental principles of how objects move and interact, specifically focusing on the mechanics of impact (percussion). He also begins an extensive categorization of scientific and philosophical terms, creating a linguistic framework for his observations.</meta>

### On Impact

No thing moves by itself if it is not moved by another; this is because it does not move within itself if it were not through the help <note>original: "aup[...]", likely *aiuto* (help/aid)</note> of that impact, which, not being in itself, cannot be the cause of its own movement, just as it cannot be the cause of itself.

No body that has motion in itself can be the cause of the motion of others within itself, unless it were by <term>accidental motion</term> <note>In the physics of Leonardo's time, "accidental motion" referred to movement forced upon an object by an external power, contrasted with "natural motion," such as a heavy object falling toward the earth.</note> given by another.

All accidental motions are not perpetual if they are not maintained by the author of the natural motion.

Because no impact occurs unless it is born from a body that has traveled motion <note>original: "moto p[er]corso"</note>, and because every accidental motion by itself is finished and falls and fails if it is not maintained, whence impact <unclear>illegible</unclear>

---

--- Page 251 ---
<meta>This page continues the transcription of Leonardo da Vinci’s personal vocabulary lists from the Codex Trivulzianus. While the previous page focused on logical connectors (conjunctions) and the beginning of cognitive verbs, folio 45r expands significantly into the descriptive and the dramatic. Here, Leonardo collects words for natural phenomena (rivers, storms), intense emotions (grief, audacity), and technical or legal terminology. This "word-hoard" demonstrates his effort to elevate his prose from the vernacular of a craftsman to the sophisticated language of a court philosopher and scientist.</meta>

<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s linguistic exercises found in the Codex Trivulziano. While earlier pages like folio 45 recto focused on specific nouns and moral qualities, here Leonardo systematically organizes his vocabulary by grammatical suffixes. This demonstrates his method of "self-schooling," where he expands his expressive range by listing variations of root words—a common technique for a self-taught man seeking to master the formal, Latin-influenced "high style" of the Renaissance.</meta>

<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s extensive lexical project found throughout the Codex Trivulziano. Having explored specific thematic groups and grammatical suffixes on previous pages, he here transitions to a more alphabetical or prefix-based organization. This "word-hoarding" was essential for Leonardo—a man who described himself as "omo sanza lettere" (a man without letters)—to bridge the gap between his vernacular Tuscan and the sophisticated Latinate vocabulary used by the humanist scholars of the Milanese court.</meta>

# Folio 51 r. — (92, 267)

## Linguistic Studies: Prefix Variations

<note>On this page, Leonardo explores the "architecture" of the Italian language. He is particularly interested in how Latin-derived prefixes—**in-** (into/not), **re-** (again/back), **dis-** (apart/undo), and **con-** (with/together)—alter the meaning of a root word. This systematic approach mirrors his scientific method: breaking a system down into its component parts to understand how it functions.</note>

</margin>

[he] glues

unusual
un<unclear>consumed</unclear>

# Folio 52 v. — (96)

### Table 88; battle notes

If you are attacked at night in your quarters, or if you suspect it might happen, be ready to use traps that throw iron **caltrops** <term>caltrops (triboli): multi-pronged iron spikes designed so that one point always faces upward to impale the feet of men or horses</term>. If you are attacked, throw them among the enemies; you will then have time to set your men in order against the attackers. The enemies, being deceived and in great pain from their wounded feet, will be able to do little. You shall conduct your counter-attack in this way: divide your men into two squadrons and surround the enemies. But ensure that you have the soles of your shoes—and likewise the horses their shoes—prepared as I described before; otherwise, the caltrops will not distinguish between your own men and the enemy. Make sure that each trap throws a cartload of these caltrops.

->**To protect oneself from caltrops**<-

If you wear, inside the sole of your shoe, a secondary sole made of cloth woven from cotton cords one finger thick, you will be safe from caltrops, as they will not pierce your feet.

If you wish to be safe from **soft soap** <note>original: "sabon tenero"; a common naval tactic involved spreading soap or grease on the decks of galleys to make them too slippery for boarding parties to stand.</note> on the galleys, keep coarse river sand scattered along the gangway and where the feet are placed; the pitch will hold it firm. Keep sacks of it always ready for whenever it is needed.

| spiritual | power | division | to imagine |
|---|---|---|---|
| particular | unique | to draw up articles | to imprison |
| affection | will | concupiscible <term>concupiscible: a philosophical term referring to the faculty of the soul driven by desires or appetites</term> | terrible |
| necessity | confusion | sentiment | presence |
| frequency | public | motor <note>original: "motore"; in a Renaissance context, this often refers to the "prime mover" or the source of motion.</note> | audacity |
| sanctity | destroyed | authority | innumerable |
| venerable | notable | hidden | to conjecture |
| liberality | to converge | covered | ignominious |
| to annihilate | contradiction | simple | richness |
| distinct | regiment | innocent | fidelity |
| valiant | government | to remember | extermination |
| | free will | to trust | deceivable |
| | to manifest | voluble | to tremble |

While the last page focused on personal defense (caltrops and naval safety), this page shifts to the construction of larger mobile defensive structures, specifically a "bancha" (bench or gallery) used for crossing ditches, and concludes with a poetic, evocative list of vocabulary intended to describe the chaos and sensory intensity of a battlefield.</meta>

# Battle notes <margin>action</margin>

A hinged flap should be made of planks nailed together and covered with cowhide; on the inside, there should be tow <note>original: "stoppa"; unrefined flax or hemp fiber used for padding</note>, and over that, a reinforcement of alder wood and an iron bar. It should be securely nailed from the inside on the side of the gallery <term>gallery (bancha): a mobile protective structure or bridge used to shield soldiers while crossing a ditch or approaching a wall</term>. On the inside, make a loophole of planks one finger thick, three fingers wide, and one and a half cubits <note>original: "braccio"; a measure of length based on the forearm, roughly 23 inches</note> long. Inside, there should be a stern-brace <note>original: "bacha da poppa"; likely a structural beam similar to those used in the rear of a boat</note> for the men to place their feet against to give a great shove. On the outside, it should be plated with iron sheets one finger thick and two fingers wide, and a hook should be made on the inside to pull it.

Note that in each one of these stations there is a cat-castle <term>cat-castle (gatta): a mobile siege tower or protective penthouse used to shelter attackers</term> in which there are large stones and heavy logs to crush the enemy. From the head of the gallery above, one should pull the cord for the discharge <note>original: "busso"; literally "noise" or "knock," referring here to the triggering of a weapon or the release of a trap</note>.

The width of the ditches in which one must run with the gallery must be 6 cubits, and the depth 4 cubits. One cannot pass inside with the gallery unless it is across the level ground outside of the enemy’s range.

<meta>This page transitions from the previous linguistic exercises into practical military engineering and defensive tactics. While the previous page was a list of vocabulary, this folio focuses on the design and deployment of "triboli" (caltrops) and countermeasures for maritime and land combat.</meta>

# Table 90

You shall have those <term>caltrops<note>original: "triboli"; spiked metal devices with four points, designed so that one point always faces upward to puncture the hooves of horses or feet of soldiers.</note></term> made of cast iron at the iron foundry, using three-part molds, and then let the points be filed.

Double caltrops
Note on battle

These caltrops are to be used from a leather bag [carried] at the side of every person, so that if the hoped-for victory should turn into flight due to the enemy's strength, these, scattered behind oneself, would be the reason for slowing the speed of the horses and bringing about an unhoped-for victory.

But so that, upon returning back, this "seed" <note>Leonardo metaphorically refers to the scattered caltrops as "semenza" (seed).</note> should not be the cause of doing similar harm to you, you will have first prepared the irons for the horses in the form illustrated below. Let there be nailed between the horse's shoe and the hoof a plate of steel, thick and as wide as the said shoe—that is, the horse's shoe.

And for the infantrymen, let there be fitted to the soles of their shoes iron plates with joints, so that one can comfortably lift the heel and take steps and run as needed without any opposing impediment; and its jointing will be as illustrated here below.

Also, if at the side of each fighting sailor there were a small bag, and [the caltrops] were thrown by hand onto the decks of galleys or ships, they would be the seeds of a swift victory; but have your shoes iron-clad as said above, and filled on the bottom with tiny little spikes, so that if <term>soft soap<note>A common naval tactic was to grease or soap the enemy's decks to make them too slippery to stand on.</note></term> were thrown onto your vessel, you can remain standing.

Furthermore, if the enemy should throw powdered lime and the sharpness of that should spoil the air which goes through the breath to the lungs, you shall station four posts in four locations along the length of your vessel, with four men per post, and each group shall have a small barrel of water and large <term>syringes<note>original: "scizatoi"; these were essentially large hand-pumps or squirt-guns used for firefighting or, as described here, for dousing airborne irritants.</note></term> that force the water out through many small holes, so that the water, turned into mist, joins with the dust of the lime and pulls it down.

->syringe — iron sole for shoes — horseshoe for caltrops<-

# A method for throwing fires that cannot be extinguished by water
Crushed sulfur and charcoal with saltpeter shall always be of good weight.

<margin>of battle, note</margin>
<note>Drawings of four caltrops (triboli), weapons used to stop the advance of cavalry or infantry.</note>
-> **caltrops** <-

If you wish to throw fire with <term>bombards<note>Early large-caliber cannons used for siege and naval warfare.</note></term> or with other throwing machines, let the balls be made of dry and thin wood. And let them be full of fire-powders of the kind that are never extinguished; and with these shall be mixed <term>Greek pitch<note>A highly flammable resin, possibly colophony or a mixture used in "Greek Fire."</note></term>, pine resin, <term>petroleum oil<note>original: "olio di sasso" (rock oil); naturally occurring crude oil or bitumen.</note></term>, and other similar things. Then attach the ignition devices to them as you will see here below.

And if it should be submerged under water, this kind shall be such that it burns always, just as a candle burns inside a tube. And it shall be made of an iron ball, somewhat thin, full of powder which, while burning, keeps extending outward, shielded from the water; so that from the other side, [the water] cannot enter through the said tube.

I say that the greater the force of the vapor from the powder, the greater will be the force of the fire that you throw. And this is true because, since the ball is made of thin iron and full of burning powder—which by burning and throwing out flame and smoke will create a force to escape—it will act in the manner of a rocket and will never be extinguished.

Furthermore, I say that if you wish to make a fire of such a nature that, through compression, it ignites and throws out flames of such force that it can never be extinguished: take a piece of round and hollowed wood, and fill it inside with powder as we described, in such a way that it will throw flames. And if [the layers] are doubled, the force will double. But if you wish to make this ball to be thrown with a bombard or <term>mangonel<note>A type of medieval catapult used for throwing heavy projectiles.</note></term>, it shall be made of a thin and hollow iron ball, filled with such powder mixed with resin. And inside, attach a light to it, which shall be made of a thin and hollow brass tube; and inside this shall be fine priming powder, and on the outside, it shall be coated with <term>aqua vitae<note>original: "acqua uita"; concentrated alcohol or brandy used as a flammable primer.</note></term>, so that it is immediately ignited and will never be extinguished. And it shall be made as shown here below.

<note>Sketches of incendiary projectiles showing internal mechanics and their trajectory from a cannon mouth.</note>
ball <gloss>at left sketch</gloss>
iron ball <gloss>at center sketch</gloss>
bombard <gloss>at right sketch showing projectile exiting cannon</gloss>

<keywords>caltrops, incendiary weapons, ballistics, bombards, Greek pitch, rocket propulsion, military engineering, saltpeter, petroleum oil</keywords>

# A method for throwing fires that cannot be extinguished by water

Crushed sulfur and charcoal with saltpeter shall always be of good weight. <note>Leonardo is specifying a specific ratio for gunpowder components to ensure a powerful combustion.</note>

<margin>of battle, note</margin>
<note>Drawings of four caltrops (triboli), weapons used to stop the advance of cavalry or infantry.</note>
-> **caltrops** <-

If you wish to throw fire with <term>bombards<note>Early large-caliber cannons used for siege and naval warfare.</note></term> or with other throwing machines, let the balls be made of dry and thin wood. And let them be full of fire-powders of the kind that are never extinguished; and with these shall be mixed <term>Greek pitch<note>original: "pece greca"; a highly flammable resin, possibly colophony or a mixture used in "Greek Fire."</note></term>, pine resin, <term>petroleum oil<note>original: "olio di sasso" (rock oil); naturally occurring crude oil or bitumen.</note></term>, and other similar things. Then attach the ignition devices to them as you will see here below.

And if it should be submerged under water, this kind shall be such that it burns always, just as a candle burns inside a tube. And it shall be made of an iron ball, somewhat thin, full of powder which, while burning, keeps extending outward, shielded from the water; so that from the other side, [the water] cannot enter through the said tube.

I say that the greater the force of the vapor from the powder, the greater will be the force of the fire that you throw. And this is true because, since the ball is made of thin iron and full of burning powder—which by burning and throwing out flame and smoke will create a force to escape—it will... <note>The text likely continues to describe the propulsion or "rocket" effect created by the escaping gases.</note>

<meta>Continuing from folio 54v, which documented psychological states and atmospheric forces, this page (folio 40v) represents Leonardo da Vinci's intensive effort to expand his vocabulary. Leonardo often felt self-conscious about being an "unlettered man" (omo sanza lettere) compared to the university-trained scholars of his time. These lists, primarily of Latinate terms beginning with the prefixes "im-" and "in-", served as a personal dictionary to help him describe complex scientific phenomena and the "motions of the mind" with greater precision.</meta>

These lists functioned as a "word bank" for Leonardo, a self-taught man who sought to elevate his prose to match the sophistication of the humanist scholars. By organizing verbs and adjectives alphabetically, he practiced his vocabulary for his upcoming treatises on painting, water, and mechanics.</meta>

Partially reported by Govi in the *Essay* <note>original: "Saggio"</note>, page 18, column 2. For the final passage, compare Manuscript **A** at the *Institute* <note>The Institut de France, which houses many of Leonardo's manuscripts</note>, folio 64 v.

The perspective drawing represents a system of offense <note>an assault mechanism</note> applied to the counterscarp <term>counterscarp: the outer slope of a defensive ditch or moat</term> of a moat.

For the notes on the *powers* <note>original: "potentie"; referring to the internal mental faculties or soul's powers</note> and the *senses*, see Richter, no. 840 — *Physiology*.

The sketch, representing the plan of an octagonal lantern <term>lantern: also called a "tiburio," this is a tower-like structure built over the crossing of a church to enclose a dome</term> resting on the four pillars of the crossing of a church and the eight adjacent pillars, was reproduced by Richter, Volume II, Plate C, figure 2.

**Table 50** fol. 15 v. — (55)

See Richter, no. 740 — *On casting*.
Compare with Dr. Müller-Walde, Vol. I, pages 227-228.

---

**Table 51** fol. 16 r. — (56)

The text of this sheet is entirely unpublished, as it was omitted even by Richter, who reported almost all the other sheets relating to the casting of bombards <note>large-caliber cannons used in siege warfare</note>.
In the *Codex Atlanticus*, drawings corresponding to the indications in this table can be found on folio 12 v. and 334 v.; the very interesting drawing on folio 15 r. should be especially mentioned, regarding the method: *of managing the core* <note>original: "del governare il mascio"; in bronze casting, the 'maschio' is the inner mold or core</note>.
Govi, in his unpublished transcription of this passage, read *small perforated ring* <note>original: "regnietto forato"</note>, but the crossed-out word does not seem to me to lend itself to such an interpretation.

---

**Table 52** fol. 14 v. — (57)

The first passage was reported by Richter, no. 1487 — *Notes on books and authors*; compare with the *Codex Atlanticus*, 117 verso, 361 verso. Count G. Porro, in the *Historical Lombard Archive* <note>original: "Archivio storico lombardo"</note>, reported the phrase regarding folly, but omitted the words regarding poverty, which form an integral part of the sentence.

---

**Table 53** fol. 14 r. — (59)

The period at the head of the sheet was reported by Count G. Porro in the *Historical Lombard Archive*: *to be corrected: "et quale" into "ecquale"* <note>philological notes on Leonardo's spelling variations</note>.

---

<meta>This page continues the catalog of Leonardo da Vinci's manuscripts and drawings, specifically focusing on technical corrections and cross-references between the "Manuscript B," the "Codex Atlanticus," and earlier transcriptions by scholars such as Porro, Müller-Walde, and Govi. The previous pages discussed casting techniques and literary notes.</meta>

->— 310 —<-

### Plate 88.
Of these <term>caltrops</term> <note>original: "triboli"; weapons made of four or more sharp spikes arranged so that one always points upward to obstruct horses or infantry</note>, <term>Leonardo</term> gives other drawings with indications on how to use them in **Manuscript B**, folio 92:
> "the murices <note>original: "mvrici"; a Latin term for caltrops</note> or caltrops are to be used in the field; to be sown in that area where one suspects an enemy assault, and also thrown among the enemies when they are pursuing their victory."

The <term>caltrops</term> are also mentioned in the description of <term>scythed chariots</term> <note>original: "carri falcati"</note> on folio 102 of the same **Manuscript B**, and they are described and drawn also in the *Codex Atlanticus*, folio 20 v.
<term>Count G. Porro</term>, in the *Lombard Historical Archive*, transcribed the first part of the passage relating to the caltrops: *correct "canata" to "carata"*.
At line 9 of the transcription given in this volume, correct the typographical error *ta* to *in tua*. The expression *tua* for *your army* <note>original: "tua per tuo esercito"</note> is often used by <term>Leonardo</term>, as in the Turin drawing reported on plate 112 by Dr. <term>Müller-Walde</term>, in the *Codex Atlanticus*, folio 20 v.: *and if you want yours to be safe* <note>original: "e se tu vuoi che sua sieno sicuri"</note>, and in **Manuscript B**, folio 10 r.: *and because of this the horses will be frightened, which, being unbridled, will turn among their own.... and will be a great hindrance and harm to themselves* <note>original: "e per questo si spaventera li cavali i quali isfrenati si volterano infra sua.... e fieno grande impedimento e dano a sua medesimi"</note>.

### Plate 94.
According to an unpublished note by <term>Govi</term>, the word <term>Milan</term> at the edge of the sheet would also be in <term>Leonardo's</term> hand, which, however, does not seem very plausible to me.

<keywords>caltrops, Leonardo da Vinci, Manuscript B, Codex Atlanticus, military engineering, scythed chariots, philology, Count G. Porro, Müller-Walde, Milan</keywords>

## Bibliography of the TRIVULZIANO CODEX

**Priest Giovanni Dozio.** — *On the writings and drawings of Leonardo da Vinci* <note>original: "Degli scritti e disegni di Leonardo da Vinci"</note>. — Milan, Agnelli Press, 1871. page 32.

**Sample of the works of Leonardo da Vinci**, with 24 photolithographic plates of writings and drawings taken from the <term>Codex Atlanticus</term> <note>a massive 12-volume set of drawings and writings by Leonardo, named for its large atlas-like size</note> — Milan, Ricordi Press, 1872 — See pages 7, 8, 9, 16, 18, 19.

**Dr. Max Jordan.** — *The Painter's Book of Leonardo da Vinci* <note>original: "Das Malerbuch des Leonardo da Vinci"</note>. — Leipzig, A. Seemann, 1873, pages 100-101.

**Charles W. Heaton.** — Leonardo da Vinci and his works. — London, Macmillan and C., 1874, page 209.

**Count Giulio Porro.** — *Leonardo da Vinci, book of annotations and memoirs* <note>original: "Leonardo da Vinci, libro di annotazioni e memorie"</note>, — Lombard Historical Archive, Year VIII, issue IV, 31 Dec. 1881, pages 688-695, with the photolithographic reproduction of folio 30 r. (59) corresponding to plate 54 of this edition.

**Ravaisson Mollien.** — *The Manuscripts of Leonardo da Vinci* <note>original: "Les Manuscrits de Leonard de Vinci"</note>. — Paris, Quantin, 1881. Manuscript A. preface, pages 13 and 19.

**J. P. Richter.** — The Literary Works of Leonardo da Vinci. 2 volumes. — London, Samson Low, 1883.

**G. Porro.** — *Catalogue of the Manuscript Codices of the Trivulziana* <note>original: "Catalogo dei Codici Manoscritti della Trivulziana"</note>. Volume II of the Italian Historical Library — Turin, Bocca, 1884, pages 207-209.

**Gustavo Uzielli.** — *Research concerning Leonardo da Vinci* <note>original: "Ricerche intorno a Leonardo da Vinci"</note>. Series II. — Rome, Salvucci Press, 1884, pages 293-296 and note on page 240.

**Dr. Paul Müller-Walde.** — *Leonardo da Vinci, biographical sketch and research into his relationship with Florentine art and Raphael* <note>original: "Leonardo da Vinci, Lebensskizze und forschungen über sein verhältniss zur florentiner kunst und zu Rafael"</note>. — Georg Hirth Munich, 1889-90.

<summary>This page lists the foundational 19th-century bibliography for the Trivulziano Codex, citing key works by scholars such as Dozio, Porro, and Richter that helped catalog Leonardo da Vinci's manuscripts.</summary>
<keywords>bibliography, Trivulziano Codex, Leonardo da Vinci, 19th-century scholarship, Codex Atlanticus, Giovanni Dozio, Giulio Porro, J.P. Richter, Müller-Walde</keywords>
